' A SCIENTIFIC JoKa—A chemist in the canton of Aargan, Switzerland, caught a number of spar- rows unusually tame, owing to the severity of the winter. He afterwards painted their feathers in the most unlikely colours, and let them ga. In a few days all the papers were full of notices of the appearance of these strange birds, and a lively dis- cussion arose in scientific circles as to the origin of these rare visitors}

A GENTLEMAN who was a great believer in the efficacy of concentrated chemical manures was chatting one day with his bailiff. " Ah, Jones," he said, "it's my firm opinion that the day will come when a man will be able to carry all the manure necessary for a ten-acre field in one of his waistcoat pockets." "Maybe, sir, maybe," responded the bailiff, "and no doubt he'd be able ' to carry all the crops he'd raise off it then in the other." A FINAL JUDGMENT.—A judge, travelling on circuit during the second half of the last century, put up at a small country inn, where, whatever might be said for the fare placed before his lord- ship, the provision offered to his horse was of a very inferior description. On the judge calling the attention of the innkeeper to the fact that the animal refused to eat the hay set before it, the host assured him that it was very good hay. "Sir," re- .. _ . _ . .

plied the judge, "in a question about hay there can ,be no appeal from the decision of my horse." - No LAUGHING Marrmß.—A courtier of the third Napoleon was being quizzed one day about the forms and ceremonials which attended Court life, _ _ . _ _ _ _ _

and asked if he did not find it difficult to preserve a straight and grave face during the many absurd and ridiculous mummeries in which he participated. "That is nothing at all," he replied; but to laugh and enjoy jokes which have neither wit nor point, to smile and to be amused when etiquette demands it, that is the worst part of our task."- EQUALLY BAD.—In a certain bank the clerks are

presided over by a rather impetuous manager, whose violent fits of temper very often dominate his reason. For instance, the other day he was remonstrating with one of them about his bad work. "Look here, Jones," he thundered, "this won't do ! These figures are a perfect disgrace to a clerk I I could get an office boy to make better figures than those, and I tell you I won't have it! Now look at that five. It looks just like a three. What do you mean, sir, by making such figures? Explain !' "I—er—l beg pardon, sir," suggested the trembling clerk, " but—er—well, you see, sir, it is a three." "A three !" roared the irate manager. "Why, you idiot, it looks just like a five!"

THE BULL AND THE GOVERNMENT PAPERS.— Some members of an English ordnance survey while in the south of Scotland entered a field belonging to a crusty old farmer. Seeing the strangers look- ing about in a way he could not understand, the farmer approached. "What are ye loitering in the field for?" "Oh we have a right to go any- where," returned one of the company. "We are surveying, and here are our Government papers. ' "Paper here or paper there," returned the farmer, "ye gang out o' my field." "No, we shan't," returned the man, "and you are rendering your- self liable to prosecution for interrupting us." he farmer said no more, but went over to his shed, which opened into the field, and let out a bull, which no sooner saw the redcoats than he went for them in full career. The surveyors matched up their theodolite and flew for their lives, while the old farmer, in great glee, yelled after them: "What are ye running for? Can ye no show the bull yer Government papers?" BUT Poou RECOMPENSE.—A family, whose home is near London, have in their employ a cook, whose ways and cooking are so near perfection, that, were she to leave her present home, on half of the mistresses in the district would be eaZer to secure her services. But, treasure that she is, she came very near to making a change of resi- dence at the close ot her very first month's stay. On the morning of the day upon which her wages became due her mistress requested her to step into the study where her master was waiting to pay her what was due. In a few moments she ruahed from the study to the kitchen, where she had left her mistress, and in less time than it takes to narrate had given that astonished lady notice. "But whatever is the :natter, Mary?" inquired her mistress, "what has your master said or done to annoy you?" "He hasn't said nuqun' " replied Mary, as she flourished a cheque in her mistress's face, "but he's on'y given me this for a month's slavery. Not me; I ain't no ortygraph collector, I ain't."

Prince Bismarck died worth a million sterling. He was rich amongst European statesmen, but poor amongst financiers. About a twentieth part of his wealth was the price paid for the long-expected memoirs, the first volume of which is approaching publication. It is said that the manuscript was divided into very small portions, so that the com- positors should not learn the authorship, but the " more sagacious " amongst them guessed the truth from the " pregnant style." That can be easily believed. Bismarck could not have written many I sentences without revealing himself in some Bis- marckian phrase. It is reported, however, that the memoirs are not really memoirs, but an elaborate vindication of the wri,er's policy without much psychological analysis of his contemporaries. In other words, there is little or no gossip. It may be doubted, however, whether Bismarck could write a book about his statecraft without some reminis- cences of his contemporaries and antagonists. Some of them are still alive, and will have a good deal to say, so that if the memoirs are not racy in them- selves they may be the cause of raciness in ethers. ' Apart from this, a review of European politics for forty sears by the chief actor on the Eurcpean stage , in that period must have the greatest historical and personal interest.

It seems to be generally understood that the Duke of Westminster's reference, in a recently published letter, to £.400,000 a year as "more than double what I receive." applies to his total income. The more reasonable reading is that it relates only to a part, for it was made in answer to the assertion that his " square Mile in the West End yields £400.000 a year," and he particularly mentions South Belgravia. The Duke has a large estate in Park Lane and other parts ot Mayfair, where the leases are falling and being renewed at greatly in- creased prices. What those are likely to be may he gathered from the neighbouring property of Lord Cadogan, where not long ago ground rents of £5 gays place to rents of £5O. The 1V estminster estate in South Belgravia has an interesting history. Now quite covered with buildings, it was to same extent a common in the seventeenth centary, the people having Lammas rights of pasture on it. It came into the possession Qf the Grosveuor family in 16.16. At the beinniug of th prent century it was marshy and anhealthy, and under the name of the Five Fields had a bad reputation a.+ d resort of footpads. Up to 1826 it was of little value to the owners, but in that year an Act was passed under whieh thet district was drained and the surface raised, and novarial with the buildings which have since become se valuable. At the expiration of the forty years which the leases have still to run its ground value will be fabulous. •

The declaration 0 the " Daily News" in favour of the reunion of Nonconformity and Liberalism on the basis of a Dis.establishment policy has caused a good deal of commotion in religious circles. It is certainly a remarkable fact that Reestablishment at the present moment is far more to the front than it has been for some years,end that the policy of the bishops has done nothing to d&ay it. In fart,the iecWnt action of Dr. Creighton,to which at- tention was drawn, in sanctioning such mediaeval festivals as All Soul's Day and Corpus Christi has drawn a protest even from the moderate Evangeli- cal sectien of the church which is represented by the " Record " newppaper. Still, I do not think that even a Dieestablishment crusade would much alter Episcopal policy. This fact is not so much due to any sympathy on the part of the bishops with ritualistic practices as to the dread syLich they entertain of a High Church secession similar to that of the Methodists at the end ot the last and the beginning of the present century. Tril espe- cially applies to London, which the " Record" de- scribes as an ecclesiastical Alsatia. The bishops, however,are well aware of the comparati•ely small number of churchgoers in London, and they are afraid that if these numbers were reduced by the secessions of the ritualistic congregations the posi- Von of the Church in London would became almost as weak as it is in Wales. It is also remarkable, considering their strong hostility to Mr. Asquith'e Disestablishment Bill, to note how indifferent the Ritualiets now seem to the raising ef the question even in England. They do say,bowever, that if Dis- establishment is to come, the sooner it comes the better.

Dereia anew and very interesting letter of Mr. Gladstone's. It was addressed to the Rev. Father Tawliabus, a Roman Catholic missionary in India, who had compiled from Dante a treatise on the divine authority of the Catholic Church, and had sent a copy to Mr. Gladstone : " Rev, and dear sir,—lt will surprise you to learn my belief that I was born and have always lived in the Catholic Church of his country, founded long before St. Auctistioe extended it, and that by leav- ing it I should commit an act of rashness and a great sin. Nevertheless, I thank you ter your most lucid communication, which I have been reading with great interest. I regard it in its motive as a manifestation of Chrittian love, and I look upon your prayer that I may be led into the right way as a favour done to me. Your great po,t (Dante) has taught ne much, and I hrpe to derive from him yat farther hem:4h before idle. And I remain, with every good wish, rev, and dear sir,yours very faith- fully, W. E. GLADSTONE. "Aug. 21, 1895."

It is thought here that the transfer of Colonel Hay from the American Embassy to the John American secretaryship of State, is not merely in- dicative of a good und-rstanding between the Government of the United Stites and that of Her Majesty. PT sonse time past negotiations have been proceeding between Washington and London at to the possihilty of the aleption by the two countries of a common policy in the Far East, or wherever else American and British interests mat/ be identical ; and the negotiations have proco.eled so far that agreement upon such a policy has sub- stantially been arrived at. Colonel John Hay is trausferred to Washington to aid in carrying out this policy, because from his particular knowledge and sympathies he is the man especially suited to the purpose. There is no intention of binding this country and the United states in an *Mance. Each country will be free to pursue its own destinies in its own way, but the Governments will act together displomatically where the common interests of British subjects and American adz :ns are con- cerned.

The eccentricities of society are always amuaing and the social philosopher might. write a volume on the changing method of greeting and of handshak- ing adopted by the beau mon6e. A new bow has made its appearance among the most fashionable members of New York society, which differs amaz- ingly from the old. Instead of a movement forward, the man turns his bead sharply at an angle, bends hi. body sideways, and in saluting the lady takes his bat off at the same angle as that in which he moves his bead, so that the hat is carried over,to a line rather below the level of the shoulder. The woman bows in a somewhat similar fashion, but in a very. nonchalant manner. Both parties to the salute incline the body to the right in passing, so that there is no danger of collision ia clops quarters, which there certainly would be if there were no " rule of the road."

One of those periodical exhibitions at the Agri- cultural Hall that mark time in our progress from old-fashioned methods of dealing with the work-a- day details of life towards the ideal when mechani- cal science is to do the work which the human hand was wont to perform was opened on Monday at lelington. This was the sixth annual Laundry vxhibition, which will last a fortnight. Otadually the laundry-maid's art, even in its mcst intricate phapoe, is being monopolised by machines so Mi- ca ely adjusted as to perform the labour of scores of hands, and PO far as the results are concerned with not a clumsy move in the whole operation. Judg- ing from the exhibits, machinery can do everything nowadaya—washing, starching, ironing, goffering, and plaiting. And yet the labourer is more than eviir worthy of her hire, for with all the modern 1 improvements the organisers of this exhibition ' declare that at the present time it 18 impossible to obtain the services of a sufficient number of rartiv sbilled hands to enable the laundry-man to conduct his bipirosen Punctually. They say, moreover, there is a vas field for respectable girls and an income which would fairly astonish those who devo'e their energies to typewriting and clerical work. By some inexplicable conjunction motor care are classed' alongside the laundry exhibits, and fill half the space of the Islington Hall.

THE EXAMINER, FRIDAY, AUGUST 26, 1898

" WOMEN'S CHAT."

The Queen's autumn sojourn at Balmoral will extend to rib-int the middle of November w hen the Court will move to Winos r. Among the many visitors Her Majesty will receive in her Scottish home will be the Duke and Daeh3ss of York, the Duke angi Duchess of Connaught, Princess Louisi, the Crown Princess of Romania, a very favourite grandeltughter, Prince and Princses Francis JosPh of Battenberg, and Countess Yon Erbsch ScLoubera, a sister-in-law of Princess Henry of Batter.- berg, to whom Her Royal Highness is most tenderly attached.

When the Queen travels to and from Seethed, a dainty time table is always provided for Her Majesty's UB9. On it are the times at which the train is due at all stations of importance, and the duration of the stop- pages. It also directs attention to those points of the route wLere anything notable or new, might interest the Rsyal traveller. After leaving Aberdeen, the mute to Bahater is extremely pretty, and notwithstanding the rainy times she has travelled over it, the Queen always Gas her chair moved to the window, that she may enjoy the scenery. After the railway journey is at an and comes the drive to Balmoral, which is lovely, and this is always a great pleasure to the Queen. In Mr. Jon Knight, Her Majo,ty has lost her oldest servant. For many years hews. head gardener at Frog more, to the Duchess of Kent, the Queen's mother, and a few m tnths before the death of the Prince Consort he was appointed bead gardeuer in Hampton Court Palace. In this post her remained until fifteen years ago, and during that perOod he'was responsible for the great vine, from which he sometimes prided himself upon cutting na fewer than two thousand bunches in a season. LADIFS LETTER

MY Dian Mama. The present is a very quiet time in the world of fashion, for while the bustle of the sales is over and done with, the time of buying clothes for the autumn is not yet come. Perhaps the briskest trade is that done in untrimmed millinery, for most people can "do " with an extra hat or two, and many possessed themselves at the sales of sheep lengths of muslin, lace, and ribbon, which elm be usefully employed in the adornment of the marvellously inexpensive straw shapes that are piled up on the shop counters just now.

One of the prettiest materials for trimming hats Is spotted ecru muslin, and a scarf of this, edged with narrow lace of the same tone, can be quickly and effectively draped round the low crown of a cream straw shape, twisted into a couple of high' loops at the side, and a finishing touch put to the whole by two clusters of cherries or bunches of poppies—one placed amid the loops at the side of the crown, the other tucked under the brim at the back. _

White spotted muslin looks well either on a white shape or one that is of the dark sunburnt tint. It should be carelessly draped round the crown in the way just described, and either a couple of white wings with a spray of flowers added at the side. Green wheat looks very cool and pretty against the white muslin, either alone or raingled with orange and white poppies. For hard wear, the newly-introduced waterproof moird is a useful hat trimming, but it is nut particularly in- expensive.

_ Although many gowns of Parisian origin are wondrously and fearfully elaborate, yet there are many whose chief charm lies in the simplicity of eut, allied to a striking combination of colour or material. A gown which is perfectly straight- forward and presents no great difficulty, but in Which there are certain points which must be care- fully preserved, otherwise the chief style is lost, is of periwinkle-blue voile, mounted on glace silk, the skirt plain and finished inside with pinked-out frills of the silk. The trimming consists of black and steel sequin net, cot into a scalloped border, and laid on petunia silk, edged with narrow petunia ruches of frayed silk. These scalloped borders are used for trimming throughout the gown, one edge finished with the petunia ruche, which is most effective on the blue 'mile. The bodice is made on a tight-fitting lining, which must fit accurately, and the top is faced with white glace silk, covered with a dull ecru guipure. The sleeves are of the glac6 silk, covered with lacel and fit closely into the armhole without any fulness, thus giving the effect of being cut in one with the bodice. The lower part of the bodice is cut in pinafore style, the back quite tight to the figure, and form- ing a slight point in the centre of the waist. Round the waist there is a shaped band of the sequin net, bound with petunia silk, and festered down on to the bodice, which is worn over the sleeves. The front of the pinafore overpart fastens under the left side, just in front of the underarm seam, and the lace yoke top under the shoulder strap. The edge of the voile bodice is outlined With the scalloped arrangement, and this in a 'slightly narrower width crosses the sleeves.

A very elegant robe I saw the other day was of dark grey satin, opening at the side to show a petticoat of plisse silk in a lighter grey. The upper robe simulates a triple tablier, and the bodice is arranged in a very novel series of tabs. The gown is very handsome embroidered with a velvet applique+ foliage design. For fancy bonices sleeves are sometimes cut in two sections, the top being quite separate, and this style is admirable for renovations, or to bring a pas sleeve up to date. It is also useful when two or three materials or diverse colours are em- ployed in the gown. Pouffe are now quite scanty, and not of sufficient depth to bag much—in fact, the "pushed up" effect is more fashionable, and any pouff or bouillonee is held up by trimmings or tucks. The small pouff only goes across the upper sleeve, and is sewn on in vandyke form, and meets the sleeve which runs up to a point,and is fastened by a fancy button or rosette of Übe velvet. The cuff also defines a point, and this can be finished With frills or pleatings. Your loving. JENNIE.

TESTING AN OVEN. —Before putting a cake in the oven sprinkle a pinch of flour on the bottom. If in one minute this turns a bright golden brown the oven is of the right heat; if it turns black it is too hot; if it is a dirty yellow colour the oven is too cooL How To CLEAN GLASS.—A little turpentine dis- solved in warm water is the best thing to wash window glass, mirrors, or glass globes. A little alcohol will also do wonders in brightening glass. Turpentine is excellent for washing sinks which have become dull and dirty.

A WARD OF THE KING.

BY KATHARINE S. MACQUOID, Author of "Maisie Derrick," "The Story of Lois," "Patty," "Louisa," "At the Red Glove," "Miss Eyon of Eyoncourt," &c.

===== 0002601_18980826_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
CHAPTER XVII. IX TUX WOOD.

Jeanne was glad when they at last reached the hostel of the Fleur de Lys. She saw that the sound of their arrival had drawn to the door an old, respectably-clad woman, evidently the hostess, and as evidently a widow. She was all courtesy and smiling welcome to the lady and he'T women, but when Raoul asked for lodging for himself and his men she told him decidedly that she could not give if them; she could barely find chambers for' the lady and her womenfolk. 'Moreover, men will; not please themselves in my quiet houie," she said. • "The village is barely half a mile away; the land- lord at. the Rouge Dragon will be more to your mind, my man ;but my house is quieter for a lady." And Raoul, remembering that his master had especially cautioned him to lodgethe Countess as privately as possible, was obliged, after some oaths and fierce grumbling", to.ride on to the Rouge Dragon, whence he sent word to the lady that he would be near at hand to receive her commands. The weather Was singularly cold for the time of year, and though Jeanne was a hardy Breton girl, she felt the keen east wind which drove in through the ill-fitting casement of her chamber. So far south she had expected a warmer climate, and she saw by the shivering ways of her old hostess that the temperature was unusual. The Countess had reached the Fleur de Lys two days ago; it stood some way from the road, had a stretch of waste land before it, and a wood on some rising ground behind. Jeanne had tried to talk to the old, frail-looking hostess, but she only answered in monosyllables, and quickly found some excuse for leaving the lady. Anne and Mathurine complained that the big, coarse-featured woman-servant, Mariette, might as well be.a dumb brute, for she could not understand what they said, and her patois was to them unin- • telligibre. An old man and a couple of lads made up the silent household; 80 silent was it that at the end of these two days Jeanne told I erself she could not bear It much longer; it seemed as though mistress and servants held some weighty secret in their keeping, which lack of silence would betray. She wished ardently that Laval would return; her ang;er at his sudden departure had soon fled, she thought it was so probable that a sudden summons had called him to Lyons. More than once since they left Moulins she had seen a man ride up to the Count, and after a few minutes talk, ride away again. Her woman Anne had been, and was still, dull and listless; she was, Jeanne thought, either fretting after Gaspard or sulky at her mistress's disapproval of her behaviour. Mathurine was by nature heavy and dull-witted; she had none of Anne's gaiety, and sharp-wittedness.. Life was duller' than Jeanne had ever known it, and on the afternoon of the third day she told her maids she .would walk to the I wood' on the rising ground behind the house, I and bade them accompany her. .She wrapped her- self in a long black riding-cloak; it made her feel happier to have some semblance of mourning about I her. 'ln these last uneventful days she had missed her loved mother more than -erer ; and without Gillorine she found it doubli: forlorn. Anne and Mathurine also felt chilly, and they had also wrapped themselves in their cloaks, so that as they moved about among the closelY;-planted chest- nut trees of' the wood the three figure', were • almost' invisible. Jeanne wondered', ihere the village was. The leader of the escort came up daily to the inn to inquire for the Countess, and to say that he had not heard from his leader. Jeanne observed that he never added the full title, and she • wondered at the mystery. In one corner of the wood she espied an sopen glade, freer from trees, and filled with scanty tufts of bracken, while briars and brambles lorded it over everything else. The tender peach-coloured blossoms still lingered, and there was an abundance of black fruit on the brambles, but it tasted dry and parched, the girl thought—very inferior to the blackberries of Brittany. Jeanne went on into the wood, leaving bar women absorbed in the consumption of blackberries; she turned to look after them, and found that she had strayed from the narrow path she had at first followed. But she was fearless; a call would, she felt sure, bring the girls to her side. Something in the mysterious green atmosphere in which she found herself—in the exquisite contrast she saw between the stalwart chestnut trunks and the fan- -I.ke green leaves spreading overhead, as if they would roof the blue sky out of sight till winter came again—filled Jeanne's heart with peace, and joy unspeakable. Before her was a fallen tree, and she sat on it, glad of the pause, and glad to be alone and free from distractions, for she could think while she sat drinking in the beauty of her surroundings. While she rode beside Laval she had not fay realised her position, but since her arrival at the Fleur de Lys she constantly asked herself what it meant. Now, absolutely free from I interruption, she went further still; she asked herself why she had consented to be dragged about the country with the Count, instead or. going straight to Amboise or Lyons, and claiming Royal protection. She saw that pure cowardice had kept her silent—a fear of vexing the Count of LavaL "But why have I feared to vex him? More than once I have doubted his honesty, and have? I fear, wronged him, for I do not believe that story of the at flip , CroceuunrdeLdoyeie ; k ancl2,eetiz-:" She eaollintanhe d while her-elbovr 'rested 1n the'palm of the other. Surely, she thought, it was bette; not tp doubt anyone in the long life that lay before her.' A little sad smile stole into her eyes as she remembered her com- plaint to Rolland; she had wished, ;to be a,boy„so 1 that she might'act for herself.' And now—now a sadden light came to her: she understood, why she had been, willing to trust to the guidance of' Laval. It was a natural longing for protection, for help; not mere cowardice, rather an inward shrinking from the danger and difficulty of her desolatelosition. That thought of Rolland had brought:CW.lz' so pain- I fully the memory of his strange desertion that she I looked up, eager • to find distraction in outward objectalooked up, and held her breath-With surprise. Near her, partly hidden by the spreading chest- nut leaves, were two men. One.'vrakvery tall, the other was shorter, younger, and more thick-set. The face of the tall figure was hidden by hillarge flapping hat, but she could make out the profile' of the other face. He was speaking, but in so low a voice that, though she saw the movement of his lips, no sound reached her. She was sure that they did not belong to her escort, and she felt frightened at being so completely alone • she dared not call her women, lest her cry should attract the attention of those strangers; yet they did not look like robbers. She flattened herself against the trunk of a tree, and tried to keep still. She had the courage to keep her face turned away: the black cloak and hood she wore would be much less noticeable if she kept face and hands both out of sight. Her chief dread was lest Anne and Mathurine should tire of eating blackberries, and follow her foot- steps. But fortunately the figures bored out of sight before any disturbing sound invaded the silence. Even then Jeanne would not call to her women. She tried to find her way to them, but it was not easy ; the trees seemed to be closer than they had been; and if Mathurine had not laughed heartily when her companion pricked her hand with bramble thorns, her mistress would have been much longer in extricating herself from the green maze around her. lite young Countess met her hostess at the entrance of the Fleur de Lys, and the pucker of anxiety in the woman's face made Jeanne stop, and ask if there was aught amiss. "The gracious lady's absence terrified me," the woman answered; "there are marauders about, and pardon me," she added humbly, "it is not safe for so young and beautiful a lady to go abroad un- attended except by women." She spoke in a shifty, nervous voice—exactly, her hearer thought, as though she were telling an untruth.

•.Jeanne was disposed to return a haughty answer; and then she passed on in silence, and went upstairs to her chamber at the back of the house. _ _ .

-She liked this chamber because it was away from the noise and smell of the stable-yard; it looked towards the wood. While she sat by the case- ment late on this evening she saw two figures issue from the trees; one of them was taller than his companion, and Jeanne felt sure these were the men whose appearance in the wood had alarmed her. She had not spoken of them to her women, and as she watched their stealthy movements, she wondered if she had wronged the hostess, and whether these were the marauders the widow-

woman amazed. ilut no; tnat coma not De; they moved cautiously, but they came towards the inn, and Jeanne drew herself back behind the curtain and watched. The two figures separated; the taller one passe out of sight, and the other came forward con. fidently to the inn. Evidently he knew his way, for he walked unhesitatingly to the back entrance. She heard the voice of the hostess, then a lon whistle, and soon after the taller figure came quickly across the bit of waste between the wood and the inn, and appeared to enter the house. Jeanne listened, but there was no sound, none of the bustle which she had learnt to associate with an arrival; it was possible, she told herself, that these men were staying in the house; yet it was strange that she should not have before this noted their presence.

===== 0002601_18980826_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
CHAPTER XVIII. SUSPICION.

Jeanne's sleep was troubled by restless dreams, and she awoke later than usual, feverish and unre- treshed. The broad morning light did not disperse the trouble caused by her dreams; it seemed to her that if Laval knew what had happened he would not consider the Fleur de Lys a fit abode for her, without the presence of her escort. She had, till yesterday, rejoiced in the absence of soldiers, but she had begun to suspect the hostess, who 'warned her against marauders, and who yet received, ' secretly and in the dark, men who skulked in the ' wood in the daytime. Mathurine brought her a cup of fresh milk and a manchet of bread; while she ate and drank, she determined to consult Raoul. She would ask the hostess to take a message to him. She had seen the hostess at the door of a room which seemed from its position to lead into the kitchen, with its broad hearth and huge, red- mouthed oven. Without summoning her women, who shared a chamber next her own, Jeanne went quickly downstairs and tapped at the door of this room. As she knocked, she heard voices within the chamber: one a deep, powerful voice. She drew back in sudden alarm. The hostess opened the door, and when she saw the lady she hastily drew back; next moment she ;lad filled up the doorway with her black skirts, but Jeanne had had time to see the tall figure of a man leaning against the opposite wall. The hostess eyed Jeanne curiously. "Your pardon, lady." She closed the door. "What can I do for madame ? " Jeanne felt disconcerted and displeased ; it seemed to her very strange to have the door shut in her face by this quiet, humble-looking woman. "I will, with madame's permission, follow her to her chamber. I grieve that madame has given herself the fatigue of seeking me." Jeanne felt obliged to go upstairs. When she was again in her chamber, she said gravely: "I wish to speak with the leader of my escort. You can send a messenger for him, can you not? I would speak with him without delay." She looked at the woman and saw her flush and tremble. _

"Pardon, lady," the hostess said; "I have no one to send. My man Jean has gone to find a fowl for Madame's dinner, and Mariette is away to fetch the wise woman for my sick cow." "But there is surely a boy who can do my errand for me ?

The woman crouched as though she would sink into the floor.

"I ask the lady's pardon, but for the moment I have no one to send. When my man returns, he shall go forth to do madame's bidding." Jeanne was no longer afraid • she was not accustomed to have her will disputed. She turned from the woman in haughty anger. "The moment he returns, be sure that you do not fail to send him. He is to tell Monsieur Raoul to come to me at once, and to bring one of his troopers." There was silence ; Jeanne turned, in surprise that the woman had not quitted the chamber. She stood with her back to the door; her lips were moving, as if rehearsing words she feared to speak aloud. She looked more timid, more perplexed than ever. "What is it? " Jeanne said compassionately. "You seem troubled. Speak out, my good woman." "With the pardon of madame, it is only that I have learnt her ladyship expects friends to meet her here, and I would presume to ask madame when the persons for whom she waits here may be expected to arrive, so that "—she trembled and stammered—"so,that I may have a chamber at the Count's disposal. It is, is it not, the Count of Laval who is expected by madame?" Jeanne looked at her in cold displeasure. She could not believe that either Anne or Mathurine would have flatly disobeyed her, for she had more than once cautioned them since they left Moulins that they were not to mention either her name or that of the Count of Laval. "It seems to me," she said, "that you are in- quisitive. You would have been wiser to check your curiosity, for I cannot, even if I would, gratify it. I do not deny your assertion, neither do I confirm it; but I cannot say when the person I expect may arrive; lie may be here this evening, he may tarry a week longer. If you have not lodging for him, we will go elsewhere; he certainly will not consent to lodge in the village with the soldiers." The woman burst into tears. "I crave pardon, madame," she said humbly— "a thousand pardons. In some way I have un- wittingly given offence. Indeed, indeed, the person, whoever he may be, that madame expects will be most welcome, and I will have a look-out kept, so that I may not be taken at unawares. I beseech madame to pardon my foolish tongue." Jeanne looked grave and she felt troubled; the woman's agitation seemed to her exaggerated and out of place. "I forgive you," she said with dignity, "but I must know why you thought the person I expect was likely to be the Count of Laval.' The woman stood trembling; she pressed her bands together as though she were wringing cheese- curds, then she said quickly: "That is easy, madame; it was but a chance guess, for, as madame says, lam inquisitive, and I had heard that the Count of Laval had ridden from Moulins to Le Puy." Jeanne started; she had felt sure the Count had gone to Lyons to see the King on her behalf. She said impulsively: "How did you hear this? Who told you? " "Ma foi, madame I in my walk of life it is difficult to remember who brings tidings. Many and many a wayfarer comes in for a manchet of bread or a cup of wine, and his talk lodges, while I scarce regard his face. His name never reaches me for his words, he might be a dream figure." This was the longest speech Jeanne had heara from her hostess, and she noted the purity of the woman's accent, and that her way of speaking was superior to the other hostesses with whom she had lodged. "Do you, then, consider it certain that Monsieur , de Laval is at Le Puy ? " "That he has been there? Yes, madame; but I cannot be certain that he is there still." "You can leave me," Jeanne said abruptly. "I need not detain you longer." The sight of the woman was hateful to her, for she felt unreasonably angry. Laval had treated her like a child or a fool; he had made her think he was bound for Lyons, and yet even mere chance people knew that he had gone to Le Puy. "Good Heavens!" the girl cried. "I would he were here! I would tax him with his deception." The woman's refusal to do her bidding had made her too angry to think; but now she wished she had asked who were the men she had seen first in the wood, and then downstairs. Something told her that her hostess's strange perplexity was connected with them; she resolved to keep in her chamber all day, and to watch from her window and see whether they returned to the wood. Why should there be this mystery about them, that they could not sit in the long eating- room, but must be hidden away in the hostess a private chamber? Her vexation with Laval, and now this puzzle, so exercised Jeanne that she sent her midday meal away almost unta,sted. The piece of embroidery with which she usually occupied herself when indoors, lay idle In her lap, and when Mathurine came in to ask counsel about some stitchery on which she was busy, her lady told her she could not be • disturbed. For a strange feeling had gradually shaped itself out of the uneasiness that possessed her; it seemed to her that some pressing ; work—she could not tell what it was—day ready to her hand, and that she must be heedful, or she would pass it by. What could it be? she asked her- self as she sat watching the wood. Time passed on ; the shadows lengthened, then became lost as clouds 4athered over the sunset; almost at once darkness had begun. There was an absence of the tender live tint spreading and gradually deepening on the trees and herbage—the tint that Jeanne had peen used to in Brittany; she noticed, too, that I though it was only September the foliage looked dry, and as if it were ready to wither. Yes, dark- ness had begun, but with its advent light shone from within on the air]: s Perplexity. It seemed

I

• to dazzle her. Almost without her will, she clasped both hands over her eyes. Could it be—was this mystery connected with the story she had been told at Moulins? Ahl—She cried out in the suddenness of surprise; had she indeed been so cunt, 50 unutterably self-absorbed, that the fact had ne4 sooner occurred to her? had she actually seen the Constable and his friend De Pomporant, and mie- taken them for robbers? " Heavens I I disbelieved the story I was told, becauses I have learnt to like Laval and his flatter', and the tale painted him as infamous. disbelieved because lam a woman, and only care to believe what suits me," she thought, in the humility brought by her discovery. She bed promised the hostess of the Cceur Loyal that she would warn the Duke against Laval, and yet she had not been willing to own that Bourbon might at any moment arrive at the inn where the fugitives were, she now felt sure, in hiding. And yet she could not truthfully warn them against the Count, for she did not yet believe he would be disloyal enough to betray the Duke to the King. She sat thinking thinking —. Ahl there was danger! Laval might shut his eyes, but there were his soldiers to be reckoned with; that man Gaspard Jeanne believed to be evil- minded and dangerous. Besides, she supposed that if Laval knew the Duke to be in hiding, it would be his simple duty to capture him and take him to Lyons. She trembled with agitation. "My father's oldest friend, his best friend; ana I am leaving him to perish, when a few word/ would warn him away from this place." She reddened with shame as she recalled her harsh judgment on the woman who had evidently tried to save the Constable from capture. Jeanne now remembered her hostess's refusal to lodge the soldiers on their arrival, in spite of her evident Poverty. "And I, who used to long to be Jeanne d'Are, or some other heroine." Well, she must not daily now. She rose to summon her women, for she meant to send one of them for the hostess. A. sound made her pause; and then, instead of seeking them, she passed out of her chamber to one Close by her own. Through its open door she had that morning noted that its window looked out on the road.

i 1

Alit her ears had not deceived her! Two cloaked figures were riding away from the house; it seemed to her that they were going to Le Puy. She leant against the lattice in despairing silence, but her eyes never left the fugitives. It was lighter here than in her chamber, for the wood blocked the way on that side, and made darkness come earlier; even here she could not distinguish after a certain distance, but she fancied that the blur into which the figures had resolved themselves suddenly quitted the road .on the left, , and struck across country. ; It was intense relief that they had left the house' before Laval arrived; but Jeanne could not parderg herself that she had not given the warning. Before she lay down to rest she sent for the hostess ; she would at least do what she could. "Madame," she said graciously, "I believe your other visitors have left you. Are g they likely return to the Fleur de Lys? " The woman looked alarmed. "No, madame, they will not return ;-theyr„—" "That is all," Jeanne interrupted. "I dornnot wish .to hear about them, or even to remember that' they have passed this way. If you keep silence about them, so will I." She was greatly surprised to see the woman come, close to her; kneeling, she took Jeanne's Italia and kissed it. "My thanks and blessinn. go with madam!: wherever she may journey. it feared madame bmi seen too much, and that we were perhaps undone. May Heaven be praised for this mercy!" Then sb• said softly: "And her ladyship's women—did thef see aught in the wood? " "They have seen nothing, and they will knot nothing," the girl answered. "Go, my goo° woman; sleep in peace." THE NEW ANTARCTIC EXPEDITION.

-- Seldom has a ship left our shares on a more adven- turous voyage writes a c ,rrespondent in the' Manches- ter Guardian" than that for which the Southern Cross weighed its anchor in the port of London on Monday. Mr. Borchgrovink and a little knot of chosen companions one and all the true knight-errants ot Modena service, are setting out on their daring quest to wrest its scret from the great frozen continent that has for ceetoriee guarded the South Pole. Anont a week ago I was id y scanning the shipping from the heights of the Tower Brdee, when my gaze was arrested by a craft In the docks which even .a casual glance could distinguish from the rest cf the shipping. She was barque-rieged, but on , the main- mast—and this was the striking feature about her— hung something which at that distance looked like a huge wart. It was my first sight of the Southern Cress. The wart was the crow's nest. Closer inspection, from So IC ttbarine's Dock proved her to be a stoutly built steam whaier,paie te,d a bosiness-lik, black, relieved only by the roughly carved figere-head of a gilded seal. Her decks were littered with piles of stores and mountains of every kind of luggage. Snow shoes,Esquimaux kaiaks, sledges, and similar unusual furniture blocked every inch of her after-deck in picturesque confusion. From every coign of vantage on the ship came the doleful , notes, of doge. Dogs were everywhere. They were pro- testing from under their long wooden barracks amid- ships; they were sleeping on every coil -of rope ; they were even wrangling in perfunctory fashion at the toot of the sacred bridge. It seemed almost as if, for some fantastic whim, the owner of the cralt was trying to turn a whaler into the semblance of a yacht with the Purpose of using her for something between a curiosity ship and a floating kennel. But there was method in all this madness. By the courtesy of Sir George Newnes, who is fitting out the ship at is own expease,and whose name must alwaye be connected in henourable mention with Borchgreviek's expedition, I was 1131bied to spend several hours oa board. I arrived ia tone to take part in a singularly impressive ceremony—the farewell ser- vice of the expedition. The white surelices of the offici- ating clergymen, as they stood at a desk improvised out of a pile of boles decently covered by the bright fol is of resolute, sternly set faces of the rounded by their friends ; pti:deit ltehaed eUr as i oo t.Tha: etc; and the stalwart and grimy figures of the crew, m de a picture, framed in the sombre setting of a busy, dirty dock, that will hauat the mind for many a day. There was a touch a human pathos in the scene. The grey- haired priest, who intoned the solemn words of the ser- vice with scarce a tremor in the voice, wes taking fare- well of his sen, who had j ined as one of the scientific) members of the expedition. linmedist-ly after the simple and impressive service everything wets bustle and cc ivity on board. A train of loaded lorries brought unending contributions to the piles of stores that ' cutn- bored the eus3r- Two years' fee the crew, twenty all told, and stores calculated to last for tree years for the expeeition prep > r' numbe-isle ten in all, were being shipped. Oae Wandered how all that pile 0' equipment could ever be etorel away, despite the feverish activity in the held. The lavish suppliee of luxuries for the expedition impressed one almost more than the enormous quantity of the chief ne cis of life, the tons of flour,of salt pork and compressed vegetabies. and the hundreds of cases of lime juice. What, one wondered, would the explorers do with eight dozen betties of somebody's sauce on the grim snowfields of the Antarctic ? Its presence seem( d uttsrly incongruous, but it only showed the infinite care with which every detail of the equipment had been pl inne I. Turning from this scene, there was plenty to keep the visitor busy on the ship herself. There was the well in the stern through which the screw, if damaged by floating ice, could be hauled on board and repaired without a mmn getting his feet damp. There were the 9 lid bows, with their nine feet of solid oak,and the stoutly built WA is of the waist,strengthened against the ice by a sheaf of Ameri- can greenheart. There was the collapsible beat, which could be made ready for use in a few seconds, and the curious little steam launch, the rudder and screw of which were attached to the same swivel, so as to mak her answer her helm the quicker. Most interesting of all, perhaps, were the kajaks, frail-lookinz, cockles shells,indeed, for a man to entrust his safety to them on a stormy sea. They were built on the lines of a canoe, decked in,with a hole in the middle. Ito this the ad- venturer fits himself like a cork into a bottle, and straps the rest of the canvas deck round his middle. Hereto, whatever happens, he cannot be spilled or swamped. Most people would probably prefer to try the merit of this craft on a duck-pond rather than on the Antarctic Ocean. And last of all the dogs. They in- sisted on their due share of one's atten ion. There were 60 of them on beard, and every one desired and deserved individual notice. Twenty more who have been enjoying a summer holiday and a shave in Kent, are yet to come. They are all of the Samoyed bread, trained for harness he the sledgee,and will therefore play an important part in the ultimate success of the expedi- tion. They are net prepoaeing in appearance, but they possess strength and endurance, and have clever, kindly eyes. They are at present fat and well-liking, but are suffering severely from the heat. Bef ire long they will have no reason for complaint on this score, and it is to be devoutly hoped that their provender, the thirty tons of biscuits, especially prepared with a liberal allowance of oil, will not run short. By the time they reach home again they will be travelled dogs, for they were brought at considerable expense from Finland. whence two trainers, quaint-looking figures in their bright-coloured blouses of blanket cloth, have borne them company. May good luck go with them, for we parted the best of friends, though those who knew them say that they have an unpleasant habit of fighting to the death among themselves.

A member of the scientific expedition v-as good enough to give me a brief outline of the main porpose and plan of the voyage. From London the S sithern Cross will sail at all possible sped for Hobart. From Tasmania she will steer for Cape Adsir, on South Vic- toria Land. Here the expedition will b 3 landed and the main camp established. From this point the exoedition will strike inland to explore the country, while the Southern Cron; returns to Hobart. In the spring the ship will return, and immediately alter the expeditlen will st3rt on its journey across the interior. Mr. Borch- grevink is the only man of the party whe bai hitherto seen anything of the Antarctic revion. Some three years ago he landed at Cape Adair. His stay only tote I for a few hours, but it was long enough to fire him with the ambition to explore this vast and urstrodd .n country. Hm is by birth and parentage a Scandinavian, and by 'choice an Englishman, a well-built man of middle height and in the prime of life, and one of these daring spirits who refuse to learn the meaning of the word " sible." Mr. Eorehgrevink hopes, when once he has sur- mounted the towering ridge of glaciers which stretches 'along the coast, te strike a hoge tableland which may be practicable for sleighs for the little journey of some 1,300 miles from Cape &dalr to the South Pole.

WOMEN'S LIBERAL LIBERAL ASSOCIATION PICNIC.

Thursday last week was the daylfived for the third annual picnic of the Warrington Women's Liberal Association. The place selected this year was Bolling- ton, via Rostherne, one of the pettiest spots in Cheshire. Shortly before one o'elock the party, numbering over one hundred, assembled at the Liberal club, and were soon comhrtably seated in the omnibuses, of which there were four, provided by Mr. Magian for their accommodation. The first two were drawn each by four horses, and the last two by three horses each—quite an imposing spec- tacle. Among the number present were Ire. Armi- tage, president; Mrs. H. Roberts, treasurer ; Ntrs. Grimshaw, secretary; Mrs. Roger, Miss Dutton, Mrs. Clay, Mrs. Cook, Mrs. Rathbone, Mrs. Hill and Mrs. Walker, committee ; Mr and Mrs, C. E. Parker, (Penketh), Alderman Roberts, Coun. cillor Tionion, Mr. and Mrs. W. Roberts,Mrs Brown Miss Brown, Mrs. G. Wallington, Mrs. Ewart, Mr.- Critchley, Mr Mounfield, and Mr. Grimshaw. The route taken was along Bewsey-street, Horsemarket- street, Bridge-street, Knutsford-road, through Moo Green, and on to the quaint little village of Roe- theme, where the party availed themselves of the opportunity of seeing the church, the mere, and the surrounding view—a very pretty one indeed. At the top of the hill seats were resumed again, and in a short time Bollington was reached, and more particularly the Swan Inn, a welcome sight to those who by this time were beginning to feel that five hours were quite long enough for a fpst. A substantial tea was prepared by Mr. Stephens, and much appreciated. Afterwards a saunter through Dunham Park, and at seven o'clock seats were re- sumed again for the return journey to Warrington, which was reached in good time. The weather, an important consideration on these occasions,wns tine but dull, which made it cooler and pleasanter than that of the preceding days for outdoor recreation and enjoyment.

DROWNING itYI:ERY AT WATt- RINGTON.

On Sunday afternoon the body of a man,unknown was found in the river close to Bishop's Wharf. The deceased who appeared about 40 years of age, was about five feet eight inches in height, of ruddy complexion, with full features, brown hair and moth,tache, and of medium build. He was wearing 4 brown rough tweed Jacket. brown check cord vest and brown cord trousers. He also wore two old shirts, aid a pair of clogs, far worn, and had a black and white scarf round his neck. It is supposed he . had been in the water about a fortnight. Mr. S. Brighouse, county coroner held an inquest at the Police • tation on Mondsy evening,—James Monks &fated that he fsund the body in the Mersey, near Bridge Foot, at 2-30 on Sunday afternoon. WATER, POLO dfIAXIPIONSHIP.= -- WARRINGTON v. ST. HELENS.—REPLATED

TIE.

The meeting of the st. Helens and Warrington water polo teams in the Liverpool and District Water Polo League Championship at St. Helens Corporation Baths on Tuesday night excited great interest among the members of the two c übs. It will be remembered that the tie was played at War- rington a few weeks ago, and resulted in a draw. The match on Tuesday evening was the replayed tie. There was a good attendance, and the teams turned out as follows :—St. Helens : J. Aspinall, goal ;J. Brettoa and T. Green backs; P. Ripley, half-back; W. Preston, W. E. Raley, and J. Law, renson, forwards, Warrington: A. G. Robertson- goal; S. Beaman and A. Boscow, backs; T. Locker, half-back • A. Ashton, W. Ash- ton, and W. Creighton, forwards. Pre- vious to the first team game the Warrington and St. Helens Combination teams played a League match.which resulted in a draw of two goals each. In the other match St. Helens defended the deep end in the first half. Play was most evenly con- tested for a short time,both teams putting in some good shote,but the custodians were safe. A foul to Warrington occurred,and W.Ashton gave to Locker, who had no difficulty in defeating Aspinall. Thus reversed St. Helens made play faster, and from a foul Hilley equalised. Killey got the ball first again, and Preston swam away. Exciting play in the Warrington goal followed, but eventually Bescow gave to Locker, and he got away from Bretten, and scored the secend goal for Warrington, and this ' brought half-time, with Warrington leading by two goals to one. In the second half a fine piece of play by Preston and Ripley resulted in the latter equal- icing amid great cheering. Warrington, however, were not to be done, for immediately after Locker got possession from W, Ashton,and scored the third goal with a long shot. Ripley Swam for the ball, and, passing back to Lawrenson, that player again returned it to Ripley, who equalised. The game was most keenly contested to the finish. A splendid passing movement, organised by Bretton and car- ried on by Green. Ripley. and Miley, ended in that player scoring what proved to be the winning goal for St. Helens, for soon aftar time was called with the final result :—St. Helens four goals, Warrington three goals.

ROUND T.EtE WORLD ON A CYCLE. CONCERNING PLUCK AND PERIL.

A CHAT WITH MR. JOHN FOSTER FRASER.

Tell, slim, wiry, with not an extra ounce of fish on his Nix-feet length 01 bone; his clean-shaven features wee her-bronzed, his mouth firm, a certain hardness of manner in his speech, a man, indeed, who is cool and calculating, and of fixed resolution —such is Joha Fast q• Fraser,the leader of the little band of three cycling adventurers who have just done two years of hard bicycle riding round the world. Fraser, with his brown skin, his brown slouch hat, his brown coat, his lithe easy swing of the limbs, is very much like a racehorse. We are old friendB (writes a correspondent), and the handshake he gave me was vigorous in cordi- ality. "Yes, old fellow, if I must tell you the truth," he said, " I'm glad to get back. Not that the journey wasn't interesting; but from the mo- ment we r, ached America from Japan and felt our faces turned towards home 1 have been really anxious to sniff the smoke of London. Lord 1 how well I remember,away in the mountains of Western China, cut off by thousands of miles from civilisa- tion; how I used to lie at nights on the hill sides and look up at the old moon and think of what a ride would be like on top of a 'bus rumbling along Piccadilly." "It's a tremendous journey you've done," I said "Yes, it's a long ride," said Fraser. "But remember, we've taken over two years to di the 19,000 miles, so the daily average won't great after all. Be then's nothing else in it. It's just what anybody might do if th.y had the time, and kept pegging at it. Don't thick I'm saying that to draw compliments out of ym. I'm sick to &lath of compliments. I'm the best man in the word to say exaetly how much courage is required to di a trip of that sort, and I say none at all. I admit be- fore we stirted I had 4 gr,dat idea that if we were suc- cessful it would b. a magbiticent and glorious journey. Now it ie over I can say the chief thing about it is not how we conquered the dangers, but how easy the whole ride wag."

"But you road havp had har3shipq by tha way?" AN JO:PENCE OF LUXURY. - . .

" Hardships? rubbish !" said Fraser. with a impatient gesture. " What ara hardships ? That's what I want to know. If I hsd come home with an eye gouged out, or my spine tied in a knot, or my health ruined, I would call that a hardship. At times there was a certain ab- sence of luxury, and the food was a bit monotonous. But that's all. Crossing Central Europe we had a fairly good time. Of course, in South Russia, where the roads were soft sand and the food for ten days at a stretch eggs, and the sleeping accommodation generally the floor, it wasn't just the same as a West-end hotel, But, bless your life, you soon get used to it. Why, after we had crossed the Caucasus Mountains and we were among the Kurds in Armenia it was positively enjoyable. I know our food, breakfast, dinner, and supper, was always alike—greasy rice. There was a sameness about I it. But think of the advantages. Think of not having I to shave for a month at a time, not having to take your clothes off fir a week, and letting the inistter oflwashing yourself be a fancy. Why I haven't I worn a stiff collar for over two years. Doesn't that I make you envy me? Heaves choose my language the I first time I have to climb into a caet-iron Htarchei white shirt. In Persia we had to sleep among the filthy natives, and we used to get angry with the parasites that favoured us ;it required the ultimate burning of our clothes to got rid of them. Riding across India was a mere pleasure jaunt—l had smallpox by the way— but when we got to Burma In the rainy season, we had to push through the jungle paths and sleep night after night in our wet clothes it wasn't exacdy what one would call agreeable. 13ave you ever slept on a bamboo floor ? It's a peculiar sensation ; you get up in the morning with your body marked in red lines, like a map of C'aphaen Junetion. But you get used to really wonderful what you do rt used to. Why, I re- collect after sleeping on hard boards for several months arriving at a missionary's house in Central Ckina. He gave me a beautiful feather bed to sleep in, with luxurious white sheets. Perhaps it was delight at the paradise I was in, but, anyway, I couldn't sleep. I pitched about and tossed about for hours. it last, in desperation I got up and lay down on the floor. I went off to sleep at once.

CROSSING CHINA.

" It seems to me, FrAser," r re narked, " von were all three very reckless or very brave to go away into Upper Burma, and then strike through unknown Central China to the coast."

"Oh," said Fraser, " you're talking like the rest. Because Burma and China are a long way from here— and I wager yru know precious little about either coun- try—you think our way must have been full of perils. I used to thmk the same before I wen: there. What are IBurma and China? They are only a lot of hills and Iplains. We were among wild tribes, but we had nothing Ito fear from them, because we had nothing worth steal- ing. The Chinese used to get playfully nasty at times ; they chased us, and mobbed us, and at times we had to fight them. But we were never in any danger. There were disadvantages, of course, having no guide and not spe .king the language, and having to lire on CilitVS9 food. But they were only disadvantages,nothing more. Western China is not a good place to cycle in ; why, out of the firet 1,500 miles we had to walk some 1,200. But all the time we were climbing up and down those con- founded hills there was never any cause for fear. It was just a steady pegging on,day by day,lsl days in all till we reached Shanghai. Yet I tell you candidly that nearly every person I speak to begins gassing about the wonderful feat of crossing China. If they go on much longer I'll begin myself to think it WM wonderful.l 1 I suppose you've had your share of adventures those I two years ?" WHAT DO YOU CALL ADVENTURES ?

" I suppose we have. Do yon call hsving a fight with a lot of Cossacks and pinging Into the air with revolvers an adventure? You do? Then you'll call it an adven- ture when a lot of Moslems in Persia chased us with sticks and stones because they accused us of being Christiane. I never really knew I was a Christian till the Mohammedans stoned Ina because they said I was one. I daregay also I could spin a yarn of adventure out of the time when feur or five hundred Chinamen chmed us on board a Fmk and pet-d us till I stood up and cursed them in my most vigor MS English. I'm sure they didn't know English, but they had a pretty good idea what the revolver in my hand meant. Oh, but if you want a really thriling yarn te print, here's one that'll suit you. It was away in the snewy mountains of Persia—l'm it de we nearly sieriflcr.d our lives by losing our way I end spending a while night in a snowstorm on a hill tip 9,000 ft up, and haying nothing to eat for twenty odd ho ire—and we were trundling our wheels up a snowy I pass when all at once a great black bear came trotting 1 down the path. Oh. we were in a mighty "blue funk." The bear grunted and stopped. We stopped too. And he began nvking circles round us, wondering which was the daintieit of the trio. That lasted for two or three minutes, and then he sniffed in disgust, and quietly trotted up the hillside. That gave 1 us a chance to talk of cnsr bravery, and how, if he had come nearer we would have plugged hint with our re- 1 volvers. We went on for five minutes, and then two more bears came along. They were more scared than we, and they just took to their heels and fled. Then we met a solitary bear, fie nearly broke his neck gettine away into a ravine. Altogether within one hour we had eleven bears prancing round us—yes, eleven, that's what I've insisted upon all alone,though I may admit to you that I fancy the eleven really consisted of a stage procession of two that kept passieg and repassing. In America I used to tell the interviewers we kilted the whole eleven. That interested them. It would never have done to have confessed to a Yankee journalist we were frightened."

CHICKEN A LA "Not knowing the languages of the Far Eastern countries meet have hem a great trouble?" 66 oh, that was amusing. You've never had to get down on your haunches before a Chinaman, flap your wings,and cock-a-doodle-do to indicate you wanted eggs. It's funny, I ean tell you. I remember away on the steppes of South Russia we pulled up for the night at a little inn, and in our bad Russian ordered sonp and boiled fowls. The landlord asked if we would have the f awls zaftra. Zaftra we took to mean' after' the soup. We said yes, and sat down to wait. We waited one hour, two hours, three blessed haulm, and whenever we asked for the fowls We always were told zaftra. At last in desperation we penetrated the kitchen, and there were the fowls harn.s'ing, untouched. The idea struck us of looting in our dictionary to see what raftra meant. I There it was, plain as a pike-staff—zaftra, to-morrow. never felt mare like talerm myself into the back yard and administering a go-id kicking!' " Didn't your appearance cause a gold deal of excite- ment among the natives ? " " Rather. Oh, we had all'sorts of treatment. Every nation had its peculiarities. A friend took me to visit 1 one of the leading Chinamen, a big, fat, bloated official. My friend told him many picturesque lies about my intrepidity and daring, how I had braved many dangers, lived wretchedly for months on bad food and slept in howls and all for the love of the thing. The Chinaman looked at me curiously a few seconts and then he said, "Too muchy damn for I !" " Now tell me Fraser." I said, "is such a ride as you are just finishing, two y 'ars of cycling in all countries and under all conditiora, really good for you ?—tell the truth !"

4 ‘ DIIAIT IT MILD r

" Well, look at me" answered the adventurer," do I look like a man broken down in health ? True I'm tweuty pup& legs in weight than I was three years aeo, but I'm all the hralthier. I'm the strong enemy of the scorcher. No ma.: reides my contempt so much as the cyclist, with his head over his handle-bars who races along, sees nothing, and wit h only rue ambition, to get over as much ground as possible. I don't say the three of us have not gone in for a brisk spin on an inviting piece of road. But we've never scorched. We have always reserved our strength, and the number of can

tulles; we have made could be countrd on the fingers of one hand. Our musclis got into such splendid trim thii: tiredness was a thing we never felt. Besides, we frequently pulled up for long rests of a week,a fortnight, and even a month at a time. Before we startmci I believe some of the cycling papers rather scoffed at us because we were unknown in the wheeling world. And that's the very reason Why we've been Successful. Had we been racing men we would have broken down within the &st month, for 'we would have knocked ourselves te pieces by foroing ' the pace. No,we're not crack riders ; we are just or- dinary wheelmen fond of wheeling. Any three fellows with decent health could have done what we've done. And if you write ails thing about this chat of ours I hope to goodness you'll draw it mild ; I've had a good and interesting tirne,and I fancy I've got rnat4rial for a read- able book,but about courage and endurance and perse- verance and all that—well, it's all rot, simply rot".— Daily Cliroxicle.

The Warrington Temperance Federation are hold- ', ing a week's open-air mission. The fir,t meeting was held on Monday evening behind the Cocoa House, Floreetnarket-street. Mr. Dixon presided, and the speakers were Mr. O'Brien and Mr. 11. Taylor, P.C.M. The latter gave an address on "Alcoholic drinks not food," and made about twelve gallons of Stokos and flopkos, such as are used in hay and harvest field. The drinks were given away, and als3 abut 500 recipes for making them. Those who tasted the drinks pronounced them excellent, and some remarked th 3 it was the most practical temperance meeting they had ever attended.

TRH observant patriot, with a Are Parties Being soul above the party conflicts of Reconstructed ?

the time, sees much just now that excites thought and speculation. The ahar p lines of old parties seem to be passing away, and national interests are once more asserting their predominance. In the constituencies there are a good many signs of a change of feeling, which can hardly be regarded as meaningless or be pushed aside with levity. Reflective minds are not quite satisfied with things as they are. There is a loosening of the old party ties. It Southport, for instance, we have the Liberal Unionists somewhat restive, and the chairman of the party deelaring his thtention to support the Liberal candidate. This is one of the several symptoms of change. Wednesday's result showed its importance, and new we may perhaps have some general admission that a reconstruction of parties is quietly going on. It is sure to he said on the other hand that one swallow does not make a summer. We ad mit the fact, but we are anxious to show that the non - party man must see many things just now that indicate movement. The precise direction of the movement it is not the object of the present remarks to indicate. Our object is to suggest that the old parties are undergoing change, and that it is beginning upon many points and in many places. Questions of trade and taxation, of ecclesiastical uniformity, of foreign policy, and of national finance seem to be exciting men's minds in no common degree. It is not that there is very much special agitation by given bodies and parties. The absence of this agitation is the re- markable feature. The movement is spontane- *us. No one can quite say why it should have I arisen. The General election is still some distance off, and on each side there is a reluctance to frame programmes and to raise party cries. Neverthe- less, there is in each of the two great parties a con- siderable amount of discontent with the present condition of things, not only abroad but at home. There is a tendency to criticise leaders and to complain of indifferesee and of inaction. It is obvious that this is the prelude, if it be nothing else, to very considerable reorganisation in the ranks of the ordinary party politicians. There is every probability that it will grow, for the simple reason that so many circumstances are assisting in the change. Great names cease to exert any magic. Even a large majority does not ensure confidence. On the other hand, there are attacks which fail to move the people. A craving for new men and a new policy is very general. We think that this is symptomatic of extensive changes in the not remote future. Beyond this we are not prepared to go. Confessedly, we are but at the commencement of the work of disintegration, and it will go on for some time before reconstruction is visible and successful. But that serious political changes are in progress can hardly be denied by the patriotic observer.

Jr may be that the rain.. watet Supply, fall of these islands has been under the average of late. We believe it has been so. The winter rains have been deficient, and a spell of dry weather in the summer has given us a good deal of anxiety. If we have really had a deficient supply,it may very well be asked what are the causes, for some of them cannot be unknown. The situation of these is- lands favours great humidity, and the warm Gulf Stream assists. Rain is usually abundant in Ire- land and in Scotland, but it has been less abun- dant in England. Some observers attribute this deficiency to the destruction of our woods, nota- bly on the hills. If there were more systematic afforesting it would be better for us in maay ways. There would be labour to supply, there would be

fewer waste tracts, and there would be more timber for home use. Woods undoulatedly attract the rain,and the spongy soil of woods favours its reten- tion. Over-drainage runs off our rainfall into the rivers much too quickly. Woods would prevent this waste, as well as tend to avert the floods which often devastate our valleys after a few days' rain. The conservation and increase of our water suppli is a very considerable problem. The more pressing necessity is rather to utilise the supplies that we have for the benefit If an ever.. inereasing class of consumers. The large Owes are now having to go very far afield. Two or three cities have had to tap the English and Welsh lakes/. There is a project on foot to combine several townS and claim water-rights over a considerable area. This is a development we may expect to grow. Instead of unseemly rivalry we shall have frienr"

icily arliY plans _

_ co-operation, and instead of small Acts of I''

runt we shall have omnibus Acts. Only by _ like these can the large towns, remote from the lake system of these islands, make the best of the; supplies ia their immediate vicinity. It is in all cases desirable to look many years ahead: Towas grow, manufacturers become more exigent in their demands for water, cor'' porate bodies use more water in the streets, and the standard of domestic con -mar, tion tends to rise. If in addition to these increar ing requirements there is any proved tendenci to declining rainfall, the water question is one of the largest problems we have to face in connectiole With public health. Pure and abundant watee:l hs.s become a necessity of commercial life, and in attentive or obstinate local bodies will soon finti, that they cannot hope to escape from the consn quences of their blindness and folly. ObviolislY, the water supply ought to be in the hands of ditg community at large. Private bodies are out& place in this connection, and the national con.' science is getting sounder on this point every year'' The present East End water-famine in Londostl adds its painful experiences to the general body (44 argument in favour of public as apposed to privatt management and ownership.

A most important point was raised during tbe hearing of a case at Norwich on Saturday which had been brought into the police court by the iga; spector of factories. The offence was admitted, bu: Dr. Mills, who gave evidence in defence,stated tbs' the result of wearing the regulation masks was tO impair the eyesight of the workers. • Warrington is particularly fortunate in having 4good postal service and for this the inhabitants iere indebted to the excellent postmasters who have °esti appointed to the district. The collection end distribution of letters in the borough are ,haracterised by commendable promptitude' and It is not every town that is so favoured as to have ,teliveries of letters each week-day as is the case in ;Torrington. Increased facilities have gradually ,ssen provided for the suburbs, and one of the latest to benefit from them is groat Sankey. At the meeting of the Parish Council for this town- -Bhip on Monday evening a letter was read from uae Warrington Postmaster intimating that he had made arrangements for an additional delivery ,kid collection daily. The extention will no doubt !!`,• a great boon to the inhabitants, and it may be described as a parting gift from Mr. West, the Postmaster. It is now generally known that he is very shortly to leave the district for Sunderland, aua although his stay in Warrington has been of short duration, Mr. West will carry with him the good wishes of many inhabitants for success in his extended sphere of usefulness.

The committee of the Women's Liberal Associa- tion are once more to be congratulated on the sue- ,c.css of their annual picnic. They are a plucky little body, and deserve to b. e supported and en- couraged as much as possible. They cater more for the educational, material,. and (shall we say?) Political advancement of their .members than do ' the officials of the Liberal Association. By means 0. f public meetings, good speakers, a social gather- ing and dance in the winter, and a picnic in the summer, they do a good deal towards furthering i the cause of Liberalism n the town, and creating a feeling of" good fellowship" among themselves and friends. We would like to have seen a fairer sprinkling of the un-fair sex at the picnic last week, when the members journeyed to Rostherne and Bollington for their annual outing.

regrettable incident occurred in connection With the burial of the railway employ 6 named John William Green, who met with such a shocking 4th last Friday week.At the inquest his name was rela as" William Green " and it so appeared on burial certificate issued by the Coroner. Pre- '44r4tions were made for the funeral on Wed- but several officious persons interfered r'u told the relations of the deceased that the ;.1-Irkeral could not take place until the burial order "ad been amended by the Coroner. The result ,as that the body was not buried until the follow- day,and the position of the distressed relatives CP. be better imagined than described. ROBBING A. FATHER, AF FRODSHAII.

At the Police Court, Frorlehant, 012 Wednesday after- noon, before Messrs. A. Thimas (presiding) and John Oekleston, Edmund Warmer Cob, aged 15. was brought up charged with the larceny of £5 2s. the property of Edwin Cob, his father, at Alvanley, on the 22nd of August. —George Spruce. aged 16 years, of the same locality, was also brought up on a charge of receiving 7s. of the money knowing it to be stolen property.— Prosecutor said on the Sunday he barl £5 2s. in a cash box, which was kept in a che,t in his bedroom. On the following day he missed it, and suspected that his eon had taken it. He gave imlormatien to the police.—Mrs. Cob (prosecutor's wife) said that on th. Monday morn- ing she called the prisoner up at seven e'clook and asked him to get his breakfast. He said that he did not want any.Subsequently:he left thehouse taking his dinner with him as if going to his work,About 11 o'clock she went up stairs for the purpose of making the beds; and had occa- sion to disturb the clothes in the ci-est. She then dis- covered that the box was gone. She searched the room but could not find it. When her huiband came in they made a second search but could not find the box. After- wards she went to look for prisoner at the works but he was not there, so she came to the conclusion that he had gone off. On coming back to the house she searched her son's room and underneath the bed she found the money box broken open and the money gone. Acting Sergeant Yung stat ad, at 8 p.m. on Tuesday night he apprehended the prisoner in Dunhatu-oth-Hill and brought him to the Pollee station at Frodsham. On chargieg him he admitted the offence.—Prisoner pleaded guilty.—The father said that he did not wish to press the ease.Hia son had been a good lad up to a few months ago, and he thought that his son was being led astray by others.—The balance of the tnonev £2 03 dd had been handed over.—Sergeant Young further stated that the prisoner's father had told him that the boy had previ- ously stolen several half sovereigns—The charge against Spruce was then gone into.—Sergeant Young said he in answer to the charge said" Yes, I knew it was stolen."—Cob pleaded guilty, and stated that they both went to Fredsham Station and took tickets for Liverpool. Some days previously Spruce hid asked him about going to Liverpool, and witne,s said that he had no money. At the same time he told him there was some in his father's box and be would get that. He then arranged with Spruce to pay expenses and both would go to Liverpool. George Spruce, who also pleaded guilty said that the money the prisoner had was £4 12s. 6d. He knew the money was stolen. The Bench bound the priaonerl over to come up for judgment when called upon, and warned them that if such actions were repeated they would be severely dealt with.
