SOUTHPORT ELECTION. -- GREAT LIBERAL VICTORY. LANCASHIRE AND LORD SALISBURY'S POLICY. Wednesday was the polling day in Southport Division to fill the vacancy caused in the Parlia- mentary representation by the retirement of the Hon. G. N. Curzon, en his appointment to the Vice- royalty of India. On the whole the day peeled off very quietly in the Southport Division. Indeed, in the morning, when the peoples had gone into Liver- pool and Blanchester and Wigan for the day's busi- ness there was something approaching stagnation. One feature about the election has been the entire absence of window cards, which are generally too common. On the other hand, almost everybody, male and female, an I including the dogs and horses sported the party colors. Of these the bright scar- let of the Liberal party was decidedly preleminant in the northern part of the division. In Southport itself the proportion was three or four to one. Young ladies had their bicycles decorated with scarlet ribbons. The butcher's boy and the milkman as they drove along had Sir Herbert's colors in their caps, and a good many of the visitors who came in for the day showed their political predilections in the same way; and even in the southern part of the division the overflowing preponderance of the Toryism which had been noticed in 1895 and 1892 had evidently passed away,and the Liberal workers were more than satisfied with the way matters were going But still the election was quiet, as indeed it was likely to be, seeing that the bulk of the electors live in long rows of villas which skirt Southport and Birkdale and Great Crosby and Waterloo. And as for the trippers, they succumbed to the blandishrn,nts of the enterprising restaurant keepers. I had luncheon on the day of the election wri as a " Deily Chronicle" representative, with some men whe had corns over :nem Manchester to vote. They were Liberal Uaio iiate who had supported Mr. Curzon at three previous elections. This time they would vote for Sir Herbert and on conversation I gathered something of what was going on. "I doet be- lieve," said one,"that the Geverament will hold a single seat in Manchester at the next election. You see, we have completely lost our trade with Madagascar. I know this because I am intimately cennected with the shipping of bdanchester goods there. I know how ter- rible the lose his been. 'Then we are threatened with the loss of our trade in the Far East as well." "And do you think,"l aeleed,"Mr.Holden has many followers?" "Of course e has," replied my informant. " A man ' like that is bound to have weight," and I can quite be- lieve that statement, for, as it happens, I saw Mr. Holden and I could at once tell that he was a strong man 'in every sense of the word. ‘: My firm," said Mr. Holden, "makes 300 miles of calico in a week, and we know something about whet Lord Salisbury has done for Lancashire. I can tell you that the state of affairs has become most serious. My firm has six mills, and we have never sent a yard to Madagascar since the the French beve had possession of the island. lam the chairman of three companies, the vice-chairman of one, and a director of three others, so th at I claim to know something about Lancashire trade." "Then you would not have much time re act as a county magistrate." I said,referring to the Liberal Unionist reply to Mr. Holden's is ter" Well, it does not look much like it," hesaid. "As a matter of fact I have not the slightest desire for any such appointment. I may tell you that lately I have been entirely out of sympathy with the Tory party, and I have offered to resign three times,but my people would not let me. As a matter of fact there is no Liberal Unionist party in the Southpert division. Some have drifted over to the Tories and others have Coale back to the Liberals. The Liberal Unionists have had no organ- isation in the division since the last election. All the work hag been done by the Conservatives, and towards that work I have given time and money in the past without stint,but you may take it from me," continued Mr. Hold 12, "that Earn not the only one in Lancashire who is tired of the present Government, and that they will find out when the next election comes. Personally, I should like to see Lord Rosebery at the Foreign Office again,but anyhow we want a change of some kind. We Can't go one as we are to-dey. Foreign aff tire have never been so badly managed as they have been during the past three years." The Liberal candidate and Lade Naylor-Leyland left their temporary residence soon after eight o'clock in the morning, and mede a tour of the committee rooms at Birkdale, kinsdale, Formby, Crosby, 'arid Waterloo, returning to Southport in the afternoon, and spending the rest of the day in the bormgh. The Conservatives slowed nothing like the energy of their opponents Lord Skehnorsiale was e Inge I during the forenoon in South- port, and visited the seuthern nortions of the constitu- ency afterwards. Of election literature they displayed but little. The electors were urged to vote for Lord Skelmersdale because the Ceneervative party was the friend of the working man, and to ensure " peace, pro- gress, and prosperity." As the afternoon wore on the exc'tmentin Southport increased. Sir Herbert and Lady Neyl 'r-Leyland drove up to the Central Committee Room about six o'clock, when they had a tremendous reception from the greet crowd that had gathered. RESULT OF THE POLL. Captain Sir Herbert Scarisbrick Naylor•Levland, of Hyde Park House, Albert-gate, S.W., and Lexclen-park, Colchester, who thus succeeds in capturing the seat vacated by the Right Hon. George N. Curzon's appoint- ment to the Viceroyalty of India, which seat he had held sines 1886,1 e the only son of the late Colonel Tom Naylor- Leyland, of Nantelwyd, Denbighshire, by his marriage with Mary,only daughter of the late Mr. Charles Scaris- brick,of Scarisbrick and Wrightington, Lancashire. Hs was born on January 24, 1864, and was educated at the Royal Military College,Sandburst, entering the 2nd Life Guards in August 1882, and becoming a captain in Jan. 1891. He retired in June 1895, since when he has been on the Reserve of officers list. He is a Fellow of the Royal Geographical Society and of the Zoological So- ciety. In 1889 he married Jane,second daughter of Mr. William Satan Chamberlain,of Cleveland, 0hi0,11.5., and in 1895 received the honour of a baronetcy. At the general election of 1892 Captain Naylor-Leyland was elected in the Conservative interest for Colchester, and represented that constituency until February 1895,when, changing his political opinions and besoming a Liberal and Home Ruler,he resigned his seat. At the following general election in July he contested this division against Mr. Curzon, and was badly beaten. He is the sixtieth new Member returned to the present House' and his election upon this occasion is a gain of a seat to the Op- position, counting two votes en a division.

RUNCORN NOTES.

[Br A COILRENPaNDENr] Once again, we have to sound a chord in the minor key, in calling attention to the sad circum- stances attending the death of the little boy, Owen, who was drowned in the Ship Canal at Runcorn,on Thursday morning. It seems useless calling the at- tention of everyone to the danger of bathin4 there, for the very fact of danger ssems to have a facina- tion. Now it is high time to prohibit the practice, until something is done to make it safe for the little ones to resort there.

The champions of a certain "baths schemennatur- ally are disposed to think, that if they had been listened to not only this life, but numerous other lives, would have been saved. Possibly ! But I think that had their scheme been more reasonable, and not so magnificent; if, for instance, they had championed a more homely scheme, there would have been little or no opposition, so that, somehow or other, it can be made to work out that they have been a bit of a stumbling block themselves.

The football season is approaching and next Sat- urday (S ptember 3rd) sees the "bill 'a rolling." The Rune )rn team have been practicing on Tues- day and Thursday night, and show considerable form. Butterworth for all his years of play does not seem to diminish in vigour, and if S Houghton, C4ll be relied on for his usual share, Wigan will find that Runcorn have still a back bone to contend with, when they meet them on the 3rd at Wigan.

Still the Runeornians of the Present day cannot be expected to' run 'em off" straight off the reel, as the club is now paPsing Virougii a very critical stage, they having decided, as I mentioned the other week, to go in for orofesiionalism. After the practice on Tuesday quite n pleasant reunim was held, and the members siein to be on very good terms with tha management.

This week the coroner cpmmented very strongly on the danger of feedinzchildren in an injudicious manner, remarking that it was a practice far too prevalanr, and one that was worth more than a --ing thought.

The local police force have recognised in a really tangible manner, the civilians that assisted them in the skuffla the other day, which after all shows that the' policeman" has a heart, and a large one too, quite capable of human action. I believe that now that some of the " hostile crowd" have been made to see things in the right, light, they aCO a hit ashamed of themselves. And on the next occasion they will,andoubtedly, display a readiness Co assiet rather than hinder the police.

FATAL FALL DOWNSTAIRS AT RUNCORN.

She was in a !elating eondition,and witn ss sent for the doctor. In the meantime, they bathed the injured eye, but thought it bast not to move her until the doctor ar- rived. When the doctor arrived,in compliance with his orders they put her in bed, and about tea o'clock she seemed to recover. She subsequently bad her dinner in bed. In the afternoon his wife was injured through falling downs'airs. He again gent for the &a:ter, and subsequently further medical assistance was obtained. The deceased, however, succumbed to her injuries about ten minutes past eleven on Thursday night. Tha s'airs of the house were a very awkward indeed. Toey hsd lived in that house ever since their marriage ten years age. Hannah Brannan, domestic servant, said that she had been employed at the Man at The Wheel for five years. The last witness had had the fever 12 months ago, and since then he had been subject to fits. He had had fits as many as five and six times a day. Thera had seareely been a day that he had been free from them except the last law days. The deceased had also been troubled with hysterical fits. There was no quarrel- ling between the husband and wife, who lived very friendly together. He last saw her Wive shortly before nine o'clock on Thursday evening. She was then sinking fast. It was his opinion that death was due to brain injury, caused by the fall downstairs. THE DANGER OF OVER FEEDING.

SUDDEN DEATH OF A CHILD AT RUNCORN.

On Wednesday morning, at the Court House,Runoorn• an inquest was held by Mr. J. E Wersley, coroner,touch- ing the death of John Waring, infant child of Mary Jane Waring, of 3, Robsrt-street, Halton-rsad, Runcorn, who died from convulsions whilst in his mother's arms on Tuesday morning. Mary Jane Waring, single woman, 3, Robert-street, Halton-road, Runcorn, said the deceasei was her son aged 17 months. Since birth he had been a healthy child. She found him to bo ill on Tuesday morning about 4 o'clock. She noticed that he was strange whilst in bed, and she thought he had a fit. She got up, and brought him downstairs with the intention of going to the doctor's ; but before she got far she found that he was dead in hr arms. She intended going to the first doctor she could ; but turnel back when she found the child dead.—By the Coroner : Which doctor did you intend going to see.--Witness : Dr. McDougall. Dr. Finlay McDougall said that he had never attended the child. He made an external examination of the body after death and questioned the mother, whose evidence he had just heard. He considersd the cause of death ;o be convulsions. She had been giving the Chid bread and butter, w'fich was too strong a fool for it. It was a well-cared for child, and probably the convul- sions would be brought on by injudicious feeding as for instance giving such a tender child" bread and butter" when it had very little power of digesting it. It was a custom that was far too prevalent. The Coroner, in summing up, said that parents should be very carPful in f oeding children of such tender ago. It was not the first time that such a case had been before him, and he hoped that it would be a warning to people to be more careful in such t•Anzs in the future.--,1 ver- dict of " Death from natural causes, due ta convulsions brought on by the child baying been injudiciously fed," was returned, no blame being attached to the mother.

FASHIONABLE WEDDING AT THE

PARISH. CHURCH.

ABEL-THOMAS.

On Wednesday morning,the little hamlet of Rock Savage presented quite a gay appearance, with an unusual display of flags, bunting, and garlands, ex- hibited by friendly wellwishers, on the occasion of the marriage of Mr. Fred Abel (third son of Mr. R. Abel,Crofton Lodge,Runcorn),to Miss Lizzie Thomas ( third daughter of the late Mr. John Thomas,Clif ton, Rock Savage). Flags were also freely displayed by ' numbers of the townspeople of Runcorn, amongst whom both the contracting parties have numervus friends. Tee ceremony took place at he Runcorn Pariah Church, where a large and fashionable gathering had congregated to witness the proceedings. The officiatin clergyman was the Rev. W. Scholes. Shortly after eleven o'clock. the bride came into church leaning upon the arm of bar brother (Mr. J. Thomas) who also gave her away. She looked exceeJingly pretty in a dress of cream alpaca, trimmed with white satin ; with hat to match, surmounted by beautiful cream ostrich feathers ; she also wore a saphire and pearl broach, and carried a bouquet of lilies and orange blossoms (the gifts of the bri&groom). The bridesmaids, who were dressed in cream, with large white picture bats, trimmed with pink, carrying bouquets of choice flowers, were :—Miss Maggie Thomas (sister of the bride), and Miss Carrie Abel (sister of the bridegroom). The duties of the best man were fittingly discharged by Mr. William Burton. After the signing of the register, the newly married couple left the church amid showers 9f rice, and confetti and the good wishes of all, from Rock Savage (the home of the bridel,where the wedding breakfast was partaken of. Just at the moment of leaving the church, the sue, which had been practically hidden by a bank of clouds, burst forth in all its splendour as if in honour of the bridal party. After the reception at the house of the bride, the happy couple left accompanied by universal good wis'oes, in time to proceed to Rackpool (where the honeymoon will be spent), by the 12-15 p.m.train from Roncorn Station.

WARRINGTON.

DEATH OF MR. ALEXANDER BAIRD.

On Monday morning at 1-30 the death took place of Mr. Alexander Baird of the Crown Inn, Queen- street. The deceased gentleman, who was 48 years of age, was taken ill a week ago with pleurisy and diptheria, and was attended by Dr. Smith. He had been proprietor of the Crown Inn for about twelve years. He leaves a widow and six children. The funeral took place on Thursday.

FoLLowrNat TEE PRACTICE.

On Monday at Wigan Thomas Walsh, a com- mercial traveller, was summoned for travelling on the London and North-Western Railway from Earlestown to Wigan without having paid his fare and with intend to defraud the company. Defend- ant admitted the offence. and said he had seen other people defrauding the company, and he had followed the practice himself. The prosecuting sllicitor stated that Walsh had been under suspi- cion for some time.—Defendant was fined 20-3. and costo. _

BLINDLY'S THIINDERSTORIL A severe thunderstorm broke ever the town shortly after six o'clock on Sunday. Rain fall heavily, but the Worm passed over without having been exceptionally severe. The air was appreciably cooler for a time, but as the night settled the heat again became very oppressive,and about ten o'clock another storm came on which continued throughout the night. At times the lightning was very vivid. Rain fell in torrents, and when morning broke the sky was still overcast.

PAT'PERISM IN LA.NCASHIRE. Mr. 11. Jenher Fu-t, general inspector of the North-Western Poor Law Disirict, and Mr. W. M. Mo erson, assistant general inspector, have issued an interei•ting document,compiled from Parliamentary returns, showing the number of paupers relieved on the IL January this year, and the average number relied on the corresponding day 'of the five pre- ceed;ng years, together svi..ll the expenditure upon I in-maintenance an 1 out-relief for the year ending Lady Day of this year, and the average expenditure for the five previous years. In Lancashire, with a population according to the cani.us of 3,969,661 per- sons, the indoor paupers, excluding lunatics and va9:rants, relieved on the let January this year were 27,856. The vagrants numbered 1,625, and the lunatics in workhouses 2.618, or a total in all of the three clas,e4 ol 32,099 per•oos, The average num- ber rel'eved of the three cla,ses on the Ist January 1893-7 was 29,655, The avenge number of persons relieved outdoor on the Ist January this year was 45,887, and the average on the let January, 1893-7 amounted to 42,874. These figures are exclusive of pauper lunatics. The Proportion of paupers this year to the census population was lin 51, and the average during the correspanding five years was 1 in 55. Tha twat or indoor and outdoor cases was this year 77986,i the average during the pre- five years 72.529. The pauper lunatics not in workhouses were this year 8,263,and the average number in the preceding five years 7,776.

Sr. MARY'S CL.THOLIC CHURCH, LATCHFORD.

The annual sermons in aid of the school connec- ted with St. Mary's Catholic Church, Latchford, were preached by the Rev. Father Anselra, 0.5.F.C., on Sunday. In the morning tne preacher took for his text the words" Suffer little children to come unto me," and in the evening the subject of his dis- course was " The Ble,sed Virgin." Eleven o'clock mass was sung by the Rev. Father Folding, O.S 8., and the choir, assisted by a few friends, produced foa the first time in Warrington,Web-r's mass in E flit. A beautiful solo passage for tenor is contained in the Kyrie. The Gloria, which is of a rather bold and severe character, is compensated for by the delightful Credo, with its quaint melodies. The dignified Sanctus is followed by a spirited fugue which recurs after the Benedictus, an exquisite solo for soprano in Weber's own inimitable style. The Agnus Del and Dona Nobis form a fitting close to an altogether admirable work. The music at the even- ing service included Charles Santley's " Gaudea- musts," Mount's " Exultet,l Niurphy's Bene liction Service No 3, and Mozart's 12th Gloria (first part). ClltrltCH OFFICIALS AT CIitSEDEN ELALL.

The officials of the Parish Church paid a visit to Cuerden Hall, The'wall, by the kini invitation of Mr. R. A. and MiB3Nitylor on Satarday. The clergy (including the Rector, Canon Willis), the church- wardens, sidesmen, members of the choir,and repre- sentatives from St. George's tied Sr. Clement's Mis- sions, oacompanied by a good number of the fair sex, 125 all told, left the Parish Church ga.es about two o'clock. By the kindness of their host and hostess, the party were conveyed to Thelwall in carriages. On arrival, Mr. R. A. Naylor and Miss Naylor received the guests in the Entrance hall, when an opportunity was taken of viewing the large and varied collection of pictures and rare prints. The party then practically made themselves at home for the afternoen, some enjoying them selves with a variety of games in the ground-i,others in the bil.iard room, and others, again, sauntering over the house, in-pecting with the greatest plea- sure, the various articles of interest. About six o'clock a most enjoyable tea was provided in the Temperance Hall, where an ()op utunity was taken by the Rector of thanking the host and hostess for their very kind and generous hospitality. About half-past seven an impromptu concert was given in the entrance hall, several of Mr. Naylor's private gue•te, including Dr. Dyer of Cheltenham College, assisting in the programme, and the adult members of the Parish Church Choir contributed some songs and glees. The party drove home about half-past eight, after a molt sociable anl enjoyable outing.

FOOTBALL. NORTHERN RUGBY UNION.

Serious difficulty has arisen between some of the lead- ing clubs in the Northern Rugby Union and their players. The new professional bye-laws come into force this season, and many players are demanding increased remuneration,in many cases double the present amount. This the committees are flatly refusing, on the ground that they cannot afford it. The new bye-law provide that players must follow a bonet-ftdeoccupationtand that football most be merely an accessory to an ordinary means of livelihood. It is alleged that players are at- tempting te override this provision. In some cases last year clubs paid etch player 15s for a win, anu 7a 8.1 for a loss, and this year are offering 103 per week ; but the players demand £l. It is understood that many clubs. in the Yorkshire S.nior Competition have combined to resist the players' demands, and a deadlock has resulted. If neither side gives way, some of the best-known names in Yorkshire Rugby football will disappear, since practi- cally no player can obtain a transf r from a duo with- out that club's consent. The general belief is that one or two of the bet players will obttin their terms, but hat the bulk will have to accept the committee's ' ffer.

WEEKLY REPORT ON THE HEALTH OF WARRINGTON. Week Ending Saturday 13th August 1898. Durinz the week the following births and deaths were registered :

Births. Deaths. Warrington Registration District 56 21 The excess of births over deaths was 35. The Birth Rate was equal to 46.3 per 1,000 of the population. The death-rate from all causes was equal to 151 per 1,000, against 14.4 the average of the previous 4 weeks, and against 22.9 the corresponding period of last year. The death- rate from the seven chief zymotic diseases was equal to 5

_,•7 per I,ooo,against 1•2 the average rate of the pievions 4 weeks, and against 50 the corresponding period of 1697. The ages of mortality were- -0-1 1-5 5-15 15-25 25-65 65 np. 5 1 7 1 21

The deaths included-1 from Whooping coluzia, 6 from diarrhcea, 1 from phtlaisis, 2 from lung diseases, 3 from heart disease, 1 from injuries, 7 from all other causes. The following cases of infectious disease have been I reported during the week:—Scarlet fever, 2 cases. Total numbsr of patients in hospital for week ending August 13th,4. METEOROLOGY—The mean temperature was 61.4 Fah., and therefore I.7deg. higher than the preceding week. The maximum temperature 8311%, was recorded on the 12th inst., and the minimum temperature 42 F., on the 7th ult. The rainfall measured '44 inch. The general direction was S.W. the reading of the anemometer denoting 715 miles.

JOHN H. GORNALL, Medieal Officer of Health.

CRICKBT.

The making of huga scoresorlaile interesting enough to the batmen and the spectators, does not alwaye tend to the winning of matches. If Surrey, as must be presumed, were really desirous of beating Lancashire, they went abut it in a very foolish way. Of course it would have been hard luck on Hayward if be had been told to get out, but cricket is a game in which the inter- ests of the whole eleven and not the inlividual must be considered. As it was, the Surrey innings, which reli- ned the huge total of 834, was not over un'il half past throe en the second day. Four wickets then fell for 116, a stand by Eccles and Baker staving off further disas- ter. On Saturday morning it seemed at first as if Surrey despite their bad generalship, would win after all. Shortly after one o'c'ock Albert Ward and Hallows went to the wickets for their ascend attempt. The pair settled quietly down to d4fensive play, and at two neither was out, though enly 36 runs had been scored. When the game was resumed the Surrey men worked with extra- ordinary kermess, but every minute that slipped by re. ' duced the chance of success; and thzugh now and again a wisket fell it was with long intervals between. Bowler after bowler was tried, but still the sena and the timo advanced. Whsn Tyldesley, who followed Hallows, was out the hour was approaching five o'clock, and all hope, of victory for Surrey were over. Albert Ward with his 63 not out deserves the highest praise. For four hours he played the most patient and steady cricket against a determined attack. To his judgment and cool nerve the draw is due to no small extent. As is only too common at the Oval, a small and noisy bind of rpughs hooted and jeered at his defensive tactics, thereby show- i•g that they were absolutely ignorant alike of the rules tkat govern vied cricketers or good sportsmen. Ward paid not the slightest attention to their remarks, and , continued to qui.otly play toe game up to its conclusnon at six o'clock, when four wickets only had fallen for 210 rune.

Once again have Warrington encountered disaster,tmt it is some consolation to know that it was at the hands of a team which has not been beaten this semen. The match took place on the Arpley ground with Leyland. the homers batting first. A poor start was macy, Callon falling a virtm to Tango's fine bowling at the thir. ball. Dr. Menial pl tvel a good game, and after seem: some changes in the batters,had J. W. Bibby as partn.er The pair made a determined resistance, Dr. Smith being eventual!, caught in that slips, having 33 runs to big credit. That reliable batsman J. W. Bibby scored 21 before dismissal. Clarke contributed a well played.ll.. After the Warrington captain retired wickets fell rap.dly and the Warrington innings closed for the poor total of 77 runs. Tones performance with the ball was a good one taking 3 wickets for 23 runs.

Th 4 start the visitors made was not promising. Dr. Smith and Thewlis bowling well. H. CYCLING. The three cycling commissioners of" Travel," Messrs. Fraser, Lunn and Lowe, are expected to arrive in England at Southampton en Saturday next by the American Line's S.S. "Kensington," after having car. ried out the most remarkable cycling journey which any Englishmen have yet accomplished. They left St. Pancros Church on July 17th 1898, were received by the ring of the Belgians two days later at his palace, the Chateau de Laeken, proceeding from thence up the Rhine,down the Danube to tho Crimea,were nearly three months traversing Russia, crossed the Caucasus and spent another three months among the wilds of Persia, and nearly a similar time in riding through the great cities of North India. They then cycled from Mandalay to the Burman-Chinese Frontier, riding down the whole extent of the Yang-tse-Kiang Valley. In Jaran the Marquis Ito entertained the trio at a ministerial ban- quet. They then crospel the Pacific, reached San Fran- cisco and found not the least arduous part of their task the journey across the American Continent. Their mounts throughout have been Imperial Rover Cycles. A. great reception by cyclists is being arranged for Mon- day next, the 29La lost., at Esher at 5 p.m., and the trio are to be accompanied by a large number of their countrymen from Eiher to their original starting place.

In consequence of a number of the members of the Warringi on Cycling Club being away on their holidays, which have chiefly taken tha form of cycling tours,there were hut few representatives of the club at the Satur- duv run which was to Higher Peover. The weather con- tinuing good a number turned cut far a run to Lymrn on Tue.day. The roads were in capital condition, the slight rain in the early part of the day having laid the du,t.

FARM FIRE AT PRESTON BEEOE.

Information was sent to the Warrington Fire Station by teleizrain shortly after eight o'clock on Tuesday morn;ng, that a fire had broken out an the farm of Mr. Robert Wade, Dutton Lodge, near Pres- ton Brook. The bricade, under Superintendent Turner, tursed out with the fire engine" Major," and on arriving at the farm found a large stack of hay, containing 70 tons, well alight. Two jets were got to work from spit about SOO yards away. When the fire had been extinguished after a few hours, the stack was practically destroyed by the fire, smoke and water. It is not known how the fire originated as no buillinvs are close by, and had the stick fired itself it would have been burning inter- Inally,whereas the stack was burning on the outside. The brigade arrived back at 7.30 p.m. The damage is £2OO and is covered by insurance:

letterg to tbe Obitor.

A CHAIIPEETOUS AND ILLEGAL AGREENIENT

Sir,—l have b.ien inEructed to call your attention to an important proneunc en int which has hem made by the Committee of the Incorporved Law Society. During the settlement of a series of libel actions (arising out of the simultaneous publication in a large number of news- papers of a news-agency report) certain documents came to light which determined a defence combination of the newspaper/ concerned to bring the conduct of the plaintiff's solicitor to the notice of the Incorporated Law society. An affidavit setting forth the facts was accordingly prepared by Messrs. Lewis and Lewis, acting for the combined newspapers, and evrorn t 3 by the writer,as secretary to the Defen e Com- bination, and council were inetruett d by Messrs. Lewis and Lewis to appear for the newspapers before the Cern- mittee of the Law Society, who held an enquiry into the case. In the course of the inoeitigation which fol. lowed,it was proved to the satisfaction of the cumin, e that the solicitor whose conduct was imptiened had entered into a written agreement with his client by which he was to receive, in sat sfactien of his solicitor. and-client costs, one-third of any d images which might be recovered. The following is the text of tha agree- ment in question We are willing to act as your solicitors in recovering for you damages against the various newspapers, and as payment for our toll solicitor-and-client costs we are, out of the damages so recovered, to have one-third and to pay you the bilance in full." After a full and exhaustive enquiry the Committee of the Incorporated Law Society found, with reference to this agreement, that, "although made bona fads, it was ohampert ms and illegal and ought not t 3 have been entered into by the respondent." Holding the view that arrangements of this description are of too frequent occurrence, and that they have a tendency to foster litigation, the newseaper proprietors by whom lain in- structed believe that, in the interests ef the public, the Important conclusion of the Incorporated Law Society above quoted should be made widely known, and I am accordingly to suggest that this letter, which has been submitted to and approved of by Messrs. Lewis and Lewis, (or the sulxtince of it should be published in you: newspaper.—l am, Sir, Yours faithfully, H. WHORLOW, Secretary of the Newspaper Society, 14, New Bridge-street, London E.C.

===== 0002601_18980826_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
BAPTISMAL REGENERATION.

Sir,—l am quite willing to grant that no one can give ehapter and verse to prove the necessity of Infant Baptism. The great St. Augustine,Bishop of Hippo,telle tis that no more than a " cosjecture " of this doctrine ean be derived from Scripture. Bet let us ask ourselves this qu- stinn : is all revelation contained in the Bible ? Let Protestantism supply ths answer. The Protestalt believes many things which he eacnot prove from the Bible ! He believes, azainst the Baptist, in the validity of infant baptism, though there is no mention of it in the Bible. Why does hi believe it ? On the auth irity of the Catholic Church ! He b ilieves, against the Qu liter, that swearing under certain circumstanses is lawful, theuen the Seriptures bii us to " swear not at all " (St. Mettbeee 5-34). Why deee he believe it ? On the a ithority of the Catholic Church ! He b ilieves that the Bab oath should be observed on tne first day of the we ik, tuough thi Bible expressly says the seventh. Levit.23-3; St. Luke 2.3-56). Why does he believe it ?On the autao- rity of the Catholic Church ! He believes that meats contsinio4 bleed may lawfully be eaten, though such eating is expressly forbidden in the Old and in the New Testiment. That the " washing one another's feet" is not now a Christian obli- gation, though it is solemnly enjoined by Christ ("Ye ought to wash one another's feet" Sze., St. John 13). He believes in the Canon of Scripture' in the in- ert itisn of Scripture, in the Revelation in Scripture, sele'y on the authority of the Catholic Church ! Lastly ttae Church of England Protestant believes as "An article of Faith that the Holy Scripture containetle all things necessary te salvation" (the sixth of the Thirty Nine Articles), though on what authority he believes that no one knows, nor does he know himself. Certainly not en the authority of the Bible. The Bible interpreted by private judgment was never iateaded by our Divine Lord to be the sole rule of faith. He bade His ministers to go forth and teach and to preach ; there is no gees- tion about the distribution of bibles. He said :—Going teach all nations; teaching them to observe all things whatsoever I have commanded you. And behold I— " the God of truth"—am with you all days even to the end of the world." " He that believeth and is baptized shall be saved, He that believeth not shall be damned." (St. Mettle. 28-19 : St. Mark 16-16). So that when the Protestant affirms the Bible alone to be necessary, he contradicts Jesus Christ! Moreoves Sc. Paul tells us with equal clear- ness that we acquire faith, not by reading, but by hear- ing. He says :—"How shall men invoke Him in whom they have not believed? And how shall they believe in Bill] of whom they have not beard? And how shall thy hear without a preacher? Consequently when the Protestant asserts the Bible alone is necessary he con- 2itradicte St. Paul! Furthermore, the Protestant rule, is barred by prescription, it is discredited as a novelty ! .There certainly was a time when the Protestant rule was Inot known. The Apostles acted upon the Cathelic prin- t eiple,urgine the claim of the living teacher t) obeclienc', ! and the practice of the Church was the same in subse- quent ages. For a whole century the Chris:lin Church existed without the Bible, as we have it now; and the only guide was the living Divine Teacher, the voice of Christ in the Catholic Church. "He who hears you hears Me." There is a remarkable passage in "tones" letter which demi yes special notice. It rune thus: " The doctrines of Christianity are not based on speculations, however clever they may be, but upon clear and intelligible utterances of the Divine Word." We will, by an illustration, show how the Protestant rule of faith, leads to the " clear and intelligible utter- ance of the Divine Word." Take four honourable and good men, ministers of religion iit this town ; (1), the Rev. F. Slater, M.A., (Anglican) ; (2), the Rev. E. T. Reed (Reformed Church of England) ; (3),the Rev. J. T. Schofield, (Baptist) ; and(4),the Rev. W. H. Drumneme, 8.A., (Unitarian). Each believes in the Bible. Each, no doubt, approaches the study of it in becoming dis- positions. They all no doubt crave God's grace, and light, and assistance. Yet each one rises from his knees bolding a Walls- different, and irreconcilable doctrine. The Protestant vicar of St. James's, finds authority in the scriptures for a sacrificing priesthood, for priestly absolution, and for the real presence of Christ in the ' Blessed Sacrament. The Protestant vicar of Emmanuel, on the other hand can find nothing of the kind. " This is My Body " means one thing to Mr. Slater, and anther thin to Mr. R. riel I Mr. Drummond, who is a Unitarian, as clevet and sincere as either Mr. Slater, or Mr. Reed, takes up the inspired writings,and he can find no proof within its pares even that Christ is God. Mr. Slater and Mr. Reed hold the necessity of 1 —and for ought I know to the contrary—hold 1 that even infant baptism is valid ; but Mr. Schofield re- I 'Radiates this practice. Here verily is a house divided against itself. To sum up,Catholice believe truth to be one,and that it cannot be anything but one,and in har- mony with itself. We hold that two churches teaching contradictory doctrines may both possibly be false, but by no possibility can both be true. That they may both be true we regard as a metaphysical impossibility,a self- evident absurdity. But if instead of two, there be five or six hundred claiming te be true Churches of Christ, the absurdity becomes more glaring and monstrous. That there can be but one true church follows, not merely from the intrinsic natur3 of truth itself, but es) from the repeated and express declaration of the Divine Founder of Christianity—e g., " There shell be one fold or flock, and one Shepherd " (St. John x 16). "Be ye all one body and one spirit, as ye i are called in one hope of your canine. One Lord, one faith, one baptism" (Erb. iv 13). If unity be essential and viAlly important, what consti- tutes the band of unity P " The Bible," cry out the Protestant churches. " The living and imperishable Voice ef the divinely assisted and (becau4e divinely assis- ted) infallible Church" exclaim Catholics. The one eystem maintains true unity in a community of three hundred millions, cowistine of every race and nation, and charactir, and disposition, and language under heaven. The other system C 2 nnot secure uniry, even within a National Church, among men of the same race and country, and of the same general character and antecedents—nay, cannot secure unity upon the Most Vital points of Christian doctrine either among the people or the clergy, or even among the bishops themselves. Yours, .ltc.,

SCHRUM CORDA

EARLESTOWN AD NEWTON. I

POLICE CASES.

At the Earlestown Police Station on Monday morning, before Mr. C. B. F. Borron, the following were each fined 23. ad. and costs for being drunk in Earlestown on Saturday, viz., John Quinn, Patrick Concannan, Michael Hughes, and Thomas Killilea. EA.RLESTOWN PRIM/TM& METHODIST CHAPEL.

Special services were held in the above chapel on Sunday in aid of the Trust funds, when excellent sermons were preached by the Rev. W.H. Daven- port, of Chester. In the afternoon, a service of sacred song entitled " Tom Cobb's Temptation " was given by the choir, the narrative being read by Mr. T. Ormand. Mr. W. Banton conducted and Mr. A. Banton presided at the organ.

Sr. JOHN'S CHURCH, EARLESTOWN. Oa Sunday morning and evening, special sermons were preached in the above Church by the Rev. J. G. McGubbin, of Platt-Bridge, Wigan, in aid of the choir picnic fund. In the afternoon a short service was held, and the Viaduct band was present and played selections of music, including "Rousseau's Dream," "The Better Land" (cornet solo by J. Stevenson), and "Kyrie and Gloria " (Mozart's 7th Mass). Ihe collection in the afternoon was in aid of the Sunday School teachers' picnic fund. The congregations were not large at either service. CYCLic ACCIDENT kr EARLESTOWN. On Tuesday evening a young fellow named Samuel Mather living in Market-street,Earlestown, met with an accident whilst riding his bicycle in Park-road near the Cemetery. He was riding up the road in the direction of Earlestown, when he was run into violently by Arthur Purnell who was riding towaris Newton. The force of the impact threw Slather over a hedge into a field, and Purnell was thrown violently to the ground, both machines being damaged. Mathbr had two fingers on his lett band broken, and two others very severely bruised and his left leg injured. Purnell escaped with a slight injury to one of his hands. EARLESTOWN WOMEN'S CO-OPERAT/VE GUILD. . . .

On Monday in beautiful weathera moat enjoyable picnic was held in connection with the above guild. Upwards of 80 women and children left the headquarters of the guild in wagonettes,about two o'clock, for Abbey Lakes pleasure grounds, Orrell. The drive was of • pleasant character, and a bugler who accompanied the party heralded their approach at various stages of the journey. An excellent tea was partaken of at the hotel, and the evening was spent in boating and other pastimes, home being reached about ten o'clock. The arrangements were carried out by the committee.

EARLESTOWN FANCIERS ASSOCIATION.

on Saturday evening, the members of the Earlee- town Fanciers Association held a knife and fork tea at the Oddfellows Arms, Farlestown. A capital spread was provided. the alter proceedings being presided over by Dr. Patterson, the gathering being of a convivial nature, several short speec.,es being , delivered. During the evening Dr. Patterson pre- sented three silver medals to those members 'oho showed the best birds in the last show in various Idepartments. Mr. F. Lithgow took the medal for the best working homer in the ehow, Mr. J. T. Holt I for the best fancy bird in the show,and Mr. W. Bray Ifor the best cage bird. Toe balance sheet was pre- sented awl showed an increased balance in hand, and indicated that the Society was in a flourishing condition. The resignation of Mr. Arthur Smith as President was accepted and Dr. Patterson was elected in his place. Nolvrrox PAPER WORK'S EXCURSION. The first of what it is expected will prove to be an annual excursion for the employees of Messrs. Gillespie and Mason, the Paper Mill Newton, took place on Saturday under very favourable circum- stances. At 8-15 a special train of 17 coaches left Newtoa-le-Willows station, conveying about 330 persons, for Morecambe. The weather was all that could be desired, and after a short ramble along the promenade dinner was partaken of in the din- ing hall of the Winter Gardens. A number of the excursionists broke their journey at Lancaster for the purpose of visiting the Castle, but owing to the Assizes being held, they were unable to do so until the afternoon, when they drove over from More- cambe. After dinner a couple of toasts were pro- posed and duly honoured "The Qneen," and" Suc- cess to the firm." During the dinner a telegram was received from Mr. Gillespie enquiring as to the propose of the excursion. Speeches were cut very short ana with cheers for Mr. Gillespie and the Directors the gathering dispersed. Mr. Barton pre- sided during the after dinner proceedings. The day was afterwards spent in various ways, and after a most enjoyable time, the return journey was com- menced at 9-15, Newton being reached at 11-45. The whole of the expense was borne by the firm.

THE LATE MILE. ALLATT, NEWTON. Since the death of Mrs. Allott, of the Manse Crow-lane, Newton, which took place 12 months 1 ago,the lady members of the Congregational Church have met, and decided to erect some suLable me- molded as a token of respect for the memory ot the deceased lady. A committee was formel,with Miss Tate as secretary and Mrs. Dobson as treasurer. A. a consequence of their deliberations it was agreed' to put up a tablet, and the 'Drier for the work was I entrusted to Messrs. Williams and Clay, of Warring- ton, who have executed a tablet in the form of an open scroll. The tablet has been placed at the pulpit end of the church over the old pew in which the deceased worshipped, it is of wnite marble on a black polished slate background, and on the tablet in gold letters is the following inscription:— "Erected by the ladies of the congregation in ten- der remembrance of their friend Janette the be- loved wife of tie Rev. James Allatt, pastor of this church,who for 33 years lived and worked amongst them. On her lips was the law of kindness, she was calm, gentle, firm and wise, and the securer of many. She entered into rest August 3rd, 1897." "In thy presence is fulness of joy, at thy right hand there are pleasures evermore." The tablet was un- covered on Monday by Mrs. Evans, the Heyes,in the presence of the committee, a few friends, and the Rev. J. Allan. Mrs. Evans in very brief but suit- able terms referred to the deceased lady and hoped Mr. Allatt vir)uld accept th 3 tablet as a token of re- spect for the memory of his deceased wife. Mr. Allatt feelingly acknowledged the kindness which had prompted the ladies of the congregation in that graceful act.

A SLANDERER SUMMONED. At St. Helens on Monday, Charles Henry Lefthouse, of 33 Clyde-stret, summoned Mrs. Catherine Bridge, a neighbour, and asked the bench to bind her over and restain her from circulating and uttering abusive and malicious statements.—Mr. H. L.Rilay appeared to pro• secute, and said that was really an action for slander. Except by actions in the High Court of Justice people of the class to which complainant belonged had no remedy, but the summons bad been taken out uncl••r one of the sections of the St. Helens Improvement Act 1869, which gave the bench power to deal with people using abusive and defamatory language.—Lotthouse said be was a distil man at Messrs. Qeirk, Barton and Co.'s lead works. Defendant had rep satedly abused him, and accused him of immoralty.—odrs. Bridge persisted in some of her statements.—Defendant was bound over to keep the peace for els months and find sureties, or four- teen days.—Mrs. Bridge said she woald do the thirteen days. THEFT FROM A ST. BELEM GLOSSWORES. At the St. Helens Police Court on Weclassdase, a lad named Robert Graham, sixteen years of age, of 2, Beck I Victoria-street, St Helens, was charged with stealing a h indkerchief and breakfast, value 4d. the property of William M'Comas, another lad, aged fourteen, ot 99. Raglan-street. Inspector Steele stated that about a quarter to seven that morning the lad M'Conias went to his work at Mews. Pitkington's Glassworks, and placed the handkerchief containing his breakfast on a box near where he worked. An hour later he went for his break- fast and found it gone. He then went in search of the Iprisoner, and found him eating the breakfast. When the prisoner saw teConias coming he ran away, but the complainant gave chase and caught him whereupon he tried to assault him. Police-constable Viinnard arrived, and the prisoner was given into custody. When charged he said, " I have nothing to say." Evidence was given by M'Comas, another lad,and Police-conetable Winnard, who said that the prisener had not worked at Pilkingeon's since ;last Saturday,—Mrs. Graham, who was in court, said he was a bad boy; be wouldn't attend to his work, and he hadn't been home since Monday week.—The bench imposed a fine of 10s. or seven days.

DaNGEROUS " LARKING." '

At St. Helens, on Tuesday, before Messrs. Thomson and Michell, three young men, named John Leeson, of City Windle ; 'fhomas Taylor, if 9, Sidney-street ; and Aaron Winstanley, of 2, Windle City, were charged with stealing a horse and cart, value £2O, the property of James Hart. Inspector Stee's said the offence was Corn• mitted about 1.50 p.m. on Monday. The horse and trap was the property of M. Mart, who was a draper living at 1, Albion-street. On Monday afternoon Hart went into the Hope and Anchor Inn, Wheelie City, to have a drink with some friends. A minute or two after he en., tered the three defendants got into the trap and drove ' off. They were pursued by Mr. John Seddon, the land- lord on his bicycle, who came up with them at Moss Bank. They returned with Seddon to the Hope and Anchor. and Constable Coutts took Leeson into custody. When charged Leeson said, " I have nothing to say about it, but that lan guilty." Taylor replied," I did not intend stealing it. It was only done for a bit of fun." Whostanley said," We didn't intend stealing the horse at all. It was done for a bit of,. joke." Inspector Steele said he did not wish to press the case. The bench would be able to decide from the evidence whether or not prisoners had a felonioua intention. If prisoners had done it for a joke, it was a sort of joke that should not be encouraged, but one that should be by all means stopped.—After hearing the evidenee,the Chairman said that the heneh would allow the prisoners to go this time. He would advise them strongly to give up that system of larking. They had rendered themselves liable to be sent to the quarter sessions, where they might have been severely dealt with. It was becoming a too fre- quent occurrence for larks like that to be played, and the prisoners must be careful in future.

PRESCOr BREWSTER SESSIONS.

THE FARNWOR CH LICENSING CASE.

The annual liceaeing sessions for the Preecet Petty Sessional division was hell on Wednesday, when the magistrates on the bench were Calonel W. W. Pilkington, Mr. F. Stapleton-Bretherton, Mr. W. Daine. and Mr. J. Berry. Superintendent Baxendale, in his report, made up to the 31st ult., showed that there were 48 licensed victu- allers doing business, 28 beersellere, and seven other licensed hewsee for consumption off the premises, the total being 83. The population was 19.197, being a license to every 231 persons. During the year three licensed victuallers bid been proceeded against for offences under the Betting Act. One was up for usiog his house for the purpose of betting. Ho was committed for trial at the Liverpool Amazes, when, owing to a flaw in the indictment, it was quashed. Tw other; were up for allowing their houses to be used for the purpose of betting. These two prosecutions were withdrawn in consequence of the summonses against the bookmaker who used the houses having been dismissed. Superintendent Keighley, of Widnes, also reported on his seclen of the division. The district con- tained 110 licensed houses,d 67 public houses, 31 Irer houses for sale " on," six beer Houses for sale " off," 'other licenses 8. No licensed house had been proceeded 'against daring the year. There was a decrease of 20 in the number of persons proceeded against for drunken- ness and other offences under the Licensing Acts. There was an average of 292 persons to each licensed house. The only obj ction te the renewal of the old licenses was by Mr. Davies (Messrs. Herbert Lewis and Davies, of Liverpool) against the renewal of the beerheuse license to Jeseph Wallace in respect of premises in Church-street, Farnwerth. Mr. T. Swift barrister, instructed by Mr. E. Berry,of Liverpool, appeared on behalf of the ap- plicant,Mr.Wal'ace.—Mr. Davies said he opposed on be- half of the Vicar of Farnworth (Rev.J.Wright Williams), the Wesleyan minister (the Rev. Henry Scarres), and Mr. Joseph Robinson. of Grove Howe, Farnworth. He stated that for over 14 years the magistrates had granted renewals of the license without discussion or onposition. The excise license was not taken out from 1889 to 1897. The facts were in a nutshell,but the nut had never been cracked yet.---The Chairman : Some nuts are bigger than others. (Laught'r.) —Mr. Swift ; And it depends upon the size of the monkey very often. (Renewed laughter.)---Mr. Davies submitted that the facts which had corne to light rendered the house undesirable. No drink had been sold there for 13 or 14 years, during the greater part of which period the premises were used as a draper's shop. The man to whom the license had been renewed had not resided on or occupied the premises for ten years,although Vie law relating to beer- houses expressly required that he should really be the resident holder or oocupier.—Mr. Swift admitted that until the transfer was made by that coart to Webb there had not been any excise license taken out for ,everal years. He admitted also that for some time it was kept as a draper's shop, and that Wallace was not, for some years, a resident on the premises. Mr. Davies afterwards called Mr. Joseph ReVeson, a ratepayer and property owner ; and Richard Repel and J.mes PreFcett, residents in the locility,who objected to the license being granted on the ground that it was not required. Mr. Swift,on behalf of the applicant, argued in favour of the validity of the license And said that the police had nothing at all to say against the house. Superintendent Keighley, of Wiclueo, said the police had no objectionto the renewal of the license, and they had never had to cetnplain of any disorder in the street in consequence of the public house. In cr iss-examina- ion by Mr. Davies he said that when the license was enewed tat year he did not knew the draper's shop was a licensed house. It was after he left the court that he heard it. He had only been in Widnes a few years, whereas the license was first granted 15 years ago. There was ample accommodation in Farnworth now without the -resent license. He knew nothing against Wallace's character. If was through the Rev. J. Wright Williams that the facts about the house came out. -

The applie-an-t Wallace wa called, and gave evidt‘nce a support of his application. He admittsd that be had been fined at Widnes for an assault. The Chairman, after the maeistrate3 had congulted, sail that the license was not granted. They reproved Wallace for the manner in which be had given his evi- dence, and sii4 it was an extraordinary thing that the lic^nse was granted lalt year. They could not exactly lay the blame upon or find out whose fault it was, but they hved such a thing would not occur again.

NEWTON AND DISTRICT HORTICUL.

TURAL SOCIETY.

ANNUAL EXHIBITION.

The thirteenth annual ex—tiib,ii-o-n-Cdfruit, flowers and vegetables, held under the auspices of the above Society, took place on Saturday afternoon at Newton in a field adjoining the Newton Nurseries, in beautiful weather. For many years the weather has been against the Society, and consequently there has been a gradual but steady falling off not only in the exhibits and exhibitors, but in the attendance of the general public. No doubt the falling off in the popularity of the Show is due to some extent to the older and mo-e influential resi- dents of the district having removed to other neigh• bourhoods, and thus the exhibitors have been greatly narrowed down. Although the day was of a most desirable character, the attendance of the general public was not by any means large during the afternoon, although the exhibits were of a very fair order. In some of the classes there WU no competition, whilst in others it was very keen, and the exhibits were of a very fair order. Still the judges experienced no difficulty in making their awards. For decoration a number of plants were kindly lent by Mr. A. P. Tnorpe, Mr. J. E. Pardey. and Mrs. Smith. The judges were: Fer the open classes, Mr. Heaton, The Lawn, Aigburth, and Mr. Smsdley, Sandfield Hall, Lowton ; for the other classes they were, Mr. Wilson, Higher Tranmere; and Mr. G. Clarke, Statham Hall, Lymm. The following are their awards :

THE NEW ST. HELENS CORPORATION

ITS EFFECTS ON THE FINANC.9.'S OF THE BOROUGH.

The Town Clerk of St. Helens (Mr. W. J. Jeeves) has submitted to the Parliamentary Committee of the Town Council a highly 'wet—sting report on the St. Helens Corpsration Act, 1898, which received the Royal assent on the 12th inst. 'rhe Council applied to Parliament for the Act mainly with the object of obtaining powers for the extension an l reconstruction of the local tramways, which they had recently purchased, but in addition a number of clauses have been inserted which will be of great benefit to the town in keeping alive the powers the Corporation already possess in the matter of street im- provements, rec. With regard to the tramways, the Act empowers the Board of trade by provilional order, to authorise the Corp oration Co construct tramways outside ' the borough. The effect of this will be that the Cor- poration will be able in future to proceed by the much less expensive method. of provisional order instead of direct application to Parlia- ment. The Act extends the time for the completion of the street improvements in Church Street Bridge-street,Raven-street, Tontine-street,and Orceskirk! street for seven years, and the tione for the compulsory purchase of lands in connection with these improvements to four years. The sections with regard to streets, build- ings, and sewers give the corporation various new power!, chief among which may be mentioned the en- larged provision for exemption from street expenses, whereby not only any church or chapel, and any chapel or meeting-house appropriated to public religious wor- ship, and any ehurchyard or burial ground, but also any premises appropriated 'do the purposes of a Sunday school and whether or not used on week days as a public elem- entary or secondary sohool,or any buildings wholly used in connection with any of the aforesaid purposes respec- tively, and not used for purposes of profit, or any pr-aises partly appropriated to one or partly to other of the said purposes,will be exempted from the payment of street expenses. No new building is to ba erected within twenty-One feet from the centre of any street in- stead of within fifteen feet as heretofore. With regard to the gas estate, the Act gives power for the estab- lishment of a reserve fund. Under this head reference is made to the financial P 0 visions of the Act as affecting the gas mortgage debt, such debt having been consoli- dated, sad th 4 period for the repayment thereof fixed. The effect of the equation is that in future through a uniform period of 33 years, a payment of £359 is. 31 will be made to the sinking fund instead of varying sums for varying periods. The sums for the current year have amounted to £4Ol ss. 11d, In regard to the water undertaking, a reserve fund is also provided for, and the mortgage debt has also been consolidated, and the period for repayment fixed. The effect of this is toot in future thr ,ugh mt the uniform period of thirty- eight years a payment et £985 21. 3d. will require to be made to the sinking fund instead of varying sums,which in the current year would have amounted to £1,158 is 31. As a result,it is expected that the Melling Pumping •station will be provided without increase in the water rent, even if the water revenue should not increase. The financial prorsione enable the corporation to consch- date the whole of the mortgage debt, including the pro- vision of one sinking fund place of the fifty-three al- ready existing ; and the eeaation of the several periods for repayment of future losna. Summarised the effect is that, as compared with the old arrangements, there will during the current and several succeeding years re- quire to be paid to the sinking fund a sum of £4.386 103 41, instead of, as this year, a sum of £5,918 Os 41. The corporation have also secured powers for the further simplication of conditions with respect to the rates of the borough, whereby in future th re may be levied two in place of three tees, and there may be one audit in the place of two. The corpora:ion have also 09011 granted the power to ac-ept gifts of lan is or buildings for the purposes of parks, hospitals, infirmaries, schools, or other public useful or charitable objects for the bene- fit of the borough.

SPECIAL TRANSFER SESSION AT ST. HELENS.

A special session for the transfer of licenses, and for hearing applications for music iiCCII3BS, was held at the St. Helens Police Court on Monday. The magistr ales on the bench were the Dereity-Mayor(Councillor Alarti n) Sir Devid Gamble, Aldermen A. Sinclair, le Cook.and J. Harrison, Messrs. J. Burchall, J. Green, J. B. Leach, B A. Dronigoole, C. Sharpies. T. Glover, and A. H Michell. The following had given notice of their intention to apply for transfers :—Licensel victuallers : Bridge Inn, Liverpool-road, from Richard Griffiths to Thomas Hart- ley ; Griffin Inn, 70, C 'liege-street, from George Clare to Thomas Clare ; Albion Vaults, 100, Higher Parr-street, from John Wells to John Lyeiate ; Queen's Arms, 96, Higher Parr-street, from John Mercer to John James Lightfoot ; and Lorne Hotel, 50, Parr-street, from Peter Jackson to William Joseph Parkes. Beer- sellers c Off bemuse, 337, Mill-lane, from Robert Halsall to Sarah Halsall ; Hand and Heart, 73, Traverse-street, from James Dixon to William Joseph Owen; New Church Tavern, 3, Aslicroft-street, from Herbert W. Robinson to James Dixon ; and the Moss Rose, 337, Fleet-lane. from Peter Makin to Catherine Makin. The applicants Halsall, Owen, Dixon, and Makin did not appear, and their Cases were struck out of the list. The other applications wire granted.—Mr. S. Brighouse, of Ormskirk, applied for magisterial sanction to proposed alterations to the Hawk and Buck Inn, Peasley 'Cross- lane, belonging to Messrs. Ellis Ward, Webster, and Company. The effect of the alterations, he said, would be to separate the kitchen, from the business part of the house. The premises at present were very inconvenient both upstairs and down, and the alterations would make it much more cornfortab'e and satisfactory for the tenant. As a matter of fact the area to be used for drinking purposes would be decreased instead of increased.—Plans of the proposed alterations were sub- mitted and explained by Mr. James Gandy, arehitect, who said the alterations would cost from £250 to £3OO. The bench: suggested certain slight alterations of the plans,which were aceepted by Mr.Brighouse on behalf of the owners, and, as amended, the plans were approved. Applications for MUSIC licenses were made on behalf of the following :—Alice Adamson, Van Tromp Inn, Church-street; Thomas Clancy, Cuerdley Arms, Church- street; Joseph M'Nelly, Baths Hotel, Warrington-road; William Cro=s, Duke of Cambridge, Duke-street; Sarah Elizabeth Battersby, Red Lion, Robins-lane, Sutton; Ann Burrows. Railway Hotel, Sutton Oak; John Rigby, Adelphi Hotel, Bridge-street; and William Collins, ot the Assembly Room, Elarelshaw-street, applied for a music and dancing license for that room, which was stated to nrovide accommodation for 120 people for dancing and 250 for other parties.—The application by Mr. Collins was supported by Mr. E. W. Swiit (Messrs. Swift and Gerner),who said that lest year the time for closing was fixed at twelve o'clock at night, but that hour had been feund an inconvenient COO by members of some smaller societies who engaged the room for their social gatherings, and he asked that the time of closing might be extended to two o'clock or half past two o'clock on such occasions. Mr. Collins was prepared to give an undertaking that the extended time should not be made use of in connection with his private dancing classes.— Inspector Steel said that during the past year there had been no applications for an extension of time for Mr. Collins's room. Ile considered that twelve o'clock was very late for the young people attending there to be out and he suggested that eleven o'clock should be the hour fixed fat closing,and so make it the same hour as that for public houses. It would still be open for applicant to make applications for an extension on special occasions. If eleven o'clock was fixed as the limit it would contri- bute to the good order of the town, and tha better management of the room.—The Bench ultimately refused all the applications for music licensee with the exception of that by Mrs. Batterb 7. The application by Mr. Collins was granted subject to the room being closed at eleven o'clock, and on special occasions, if an extension of time was required, application must be made to the magis- trates.

A WIDNES MAN DROWNED AT CIIERDLEY.

Cu-s-rdley Cross was on Sunday, ihoWl-3-7--b.efore noon, the scene of a distressing bathing fatality, the victim being a young man named Henry Phasey, a moulder at Widness Foundry, who resided at 3, Roberts-street, Widnes. Poasey, in company with three other youths named Thomas Platt, John Hol- gate, and John DIViOA, selected a pond in some land belonging to the Cheshire Lines Company of Cuerd- ley Cross for a quiet bathe. The water is of con- siderable depth, and after swimming about for a while Phasey told his companions that he could swim across, a distance of twenty-five or thirty yards. After goidg about five yards Phasey was seen to throw up his hands and disappear. Reigate, who 18 a railway porter at Stanhouse-laue, gallantly dived into the rescue of his unfortunate comrade, whose frantic struggles were nearly fatal to his would-be rescuer, who was pulled down twice, and eventually had to struggle away from Phasey. The body was recovered about half-past two withi the aid of grappling irons.—On Monday night, at the Cuerdley Cross Inn, near Widnes, Mr. S. Brighouse held an inquest on the deceased. After having swum round the edge of the water deceased said he would swim across, and he asked Holgate and Platt to follow him. They did so. When he had taken three or four strokes the deceased threw up his arms and sank. Holgate attempted to rescue him, but Pheysey seized him wildly,and dragged him to the bottom. After a struggle Holgate freed him- self, but as he reached the surface Pheysey seized his feet and was dragging him down again, when Platt came to his as-astance, and it was only with the utmost difficulty that these two, assisted by Daniels, reached the bank, fully exhausted by their 'struggles with the drowning man. Pheysey did not rise again, and it was three hours before the body was recovered.—Terthet, " Accideatal death.'s

Hints on Cooking Corn Flour. • Notwithstanding the wide use of Corn Flour, particularly in summer, as a cool and refreshing Blanc-mange, many fail to bring out its true and delicate flavour. To thoroughly enjoy a Blanc-mange FIRST.—Make wholly with good sweet milk without any addition of water, and do not use too much Corn Flour. • (2i on of BROWN & POLISON'S "PATENT" Corn Flour to two pints milk is the right proportion.) SECOND.—BIend the Corn Flour with a little of the milk, bring the rest to the boil, take off the fire, and add the Corn Flour slowly. b, THIRD.—Then boil well, in fact, boil down to three-fourths of the original bulk, which will take about ten minutes' boiling. To use warm, turn out when cold, and reheat in the oven. FOURTH,—And above all, use only and always the recognised best— BROWN & P,OLSON'S "PATENT" CORN FLOUR. It is more delicate in flavour and goes further than others. One pound of Brown & Poison's Corn Flour will make twelve one-pint puddings. Thus the cost of the Corn Flour in a pudding is so small, that everyone can afford to have the best. Ask for BROWN & POLSON'S CORN FLOUR —"PATENT" is their BEST QUALITT—and be sure that you get it.
